Q^{-1}Z{Q^{-1}}^{\top}
.
But developing
A_{i}\sprod Y=A_{i}\sprod(Q^{-1}Z{Q^{-1}}^{\top})
by brute force
could be quite painful and lead to a mess.
We can obtain a more elegant reformulation using the lemma below.

Lemma 3.3.0.1
.

Given symmetric matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
and non-singular matrix
R
, the following
holds:

A\sprod B=\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}AR^{-1}\right)\sprod\left(RBR^{\top}\right)

Proof.

Using the eigendecomposition
(
1.1.1
)

can write
A=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda^{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}

and
B=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda^{b}_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\top}
. Given that the scalar product is distributive,
to prove the lemma,
it is enough to show

\left(\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\right)\sprod\left(\mathbf{b}_{j}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}\right)=\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}R^{-1}\right)\sprod\left(R\mathbf{b}_{j}\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\top}R^{\top}\right)~\forall i,j\in[1..n]
.
We can calculate

\displaystyle\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{\top}R^{-1}\right)\sprod\left(R\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{\top}R^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}\mathbf{a}\right)\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}\mathbf{a}\right)^{\top}\right)\sprod\left((R\mathbf{b})(R\mathbf{b})^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left({R^{-1}}^{\top}\mathbf{a}\right)\sprod\left(R\mathbf{b}\right)\right)^{2}

(we applied Lemma
1.3.3.1
)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{a}^{\top}{R^{-1}}R\mathbf{b}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\right)\sprod\left(\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{\top}\right).

(we applied Lemma
1.3.3.1
)

which finishes the proof.
∎

Based on this lemma, we have
B\sprod Y=\left({Q^{-1}}^{\top}BQ^{-1}\right)\sprod\left(QYQ^{\top}\right)=\left({Q^{-1}}^{\top}YQ^{-1}\right)\sprod Z
. By applying the same calculations on
A_{i}\sprod X~\forall i\in[1..n]
, we
obtain that the dual
(DSDP)
program is equivalent to:

\displaystyle[left={(DSDP_{Z})\empheqlbrace}]\max~

\displaystyle\left({Q^{-1}}^{\top}BQ^{-1}\right)\sprod Z

\displaystyle s.t~

\displaystyle\left({Q^{-1}}^{\top}A_{i}Q^{-1}\right)\sprod Z=c_{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]\hskip-60.00009pt

\displaystyle Z\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(recall
Z\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff Y\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
since
Y
and
Z
are congruent)

3.4
Convex quadratic programming is a particular case
of SDP programming

We consider a convex quadratic program with
n
variables
x_{1},x_{2},\dots x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\min~~

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}A_{0}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

(3.4.1a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}A_{i}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq c_{i}~\forall i\in[1..p]

(3.4.1b)

Particularizing Prop.
1.8.1
, a quadratic function
\mathbf{x}^{\top}A_{i}\mathbf{x}
(
\forall i\in[0..p]
) is convex if and only if the Hessian
2A_{i}
is SDP,
i.e.
, we need to have

A_{i}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}~\forall i\in[0..p]
.

3.4.1
Reformulation using the (Cholesky) factorization of SDP matrices

Each
A_{i}
(with
i\in[0..p]
) can be factorized as
A_{i}=R_{i}R_{i}^{\top}

using any of the presented decompositions of SDP matrices (
e.g.
,
Cholesky, eigenvalue or square root, see Corollary
1.7.1
).
Let us move the objective function into the constraint set by introducing
a real variable
c_{0}
that has to be minimized such that
\mathbf{x}^{\top}A_{0}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq c_{0}
.
After simple algebraic manipulations,
the above program
(
3.4.1a
)
-
(
3.4.1b
)
can be written as:

\displaystyle\min~~

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle c_{i}-\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\top}R_{i}R_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\geq 0~\forall i\in[0..p]

Using the Schur complements Property
1.3.2
, the above program is further equivalent to program below. Indeed,
by applying the Schur complement, one can easily check that a feasible solution of above program is feasible in program below
and vice-versa.

\displaystyle\min~~

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}I_{n}&R_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\\
\mathbf{x}^{\top}R_{i}&c_{i}-\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}~~~\forall i\in[0..p],

which is a program in variables
x_{1},~x_{2},\dots x_{n}
and
c_{0}
. This is an SDP program
with
p+1
constraints (that could be expressed in an aggregated form
(
2.1.1a
)
-
(
2.1.1c
)

with a unique constraint, see also Prop.
2.1.4
).

3.4.2
Reformulation by relaxing
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}
into
X\succeq\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}

We will show that
(
3.4.1a
)
-
(
3.4.1b
)
is equivalent to the following SDP program:

\displaystyle\min~~

\displaystyle A_{0}\sprod X+\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

(3.4.2a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle A_{i}\sprod X+\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\leq c_{i}~\forall i\in[1..p]

(3.4.2b)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(3.4.2c)

First, it is clear that any feasible solution
\mathbf{x}
of

(
3.4.1a
)
-
(
3.4.1b
)

can be associated
to a feasible solution

X=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}

of
(
3.4.2a
)
-
(
3.4.2c
)
that has the same objective value.
Conversely, it is possible to show that a feasible solution
(
3.4.2a
)
-
(
3.4.2c
)

corresponds to feasible solution of

(
3.4.1a
)
-
(
3.4.1b
)
, but we still need a few lines.
Any
X
and
\mathbf{x}
that satisfy
(
3.4.2c
)
also satisfy

X\succeq\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}

by virtue of the Schur complements Prop.
1.3.1
.
Thus,
X
can be written in the form
X=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}+S
for some
S\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
Since
A_{i}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}~\forall i\in[0..p]
, each product
A_{i}\sprod S
is non-negative.
This ensures the fact that
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}
satisfy all constraints
(
3.4.1b
)
and,
similarly, the objective value
(
3.4.2a
)
satisfies

A_{0}\sprod\left(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}+S\right)+\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\geq A_{0}\sprod\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}+\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
. In other words,
\mathbf{x}
is a feasible solution of

(
3.4.1a
)
-
(
3.4.1b
)
and its objective value is no worse than
that of
X
and
\mathbf{x}
in
(
3.4.2a
)
.

The above
(
3.4.2a
)
-
(
3.4.2c
)
is actually an SDP program
that can be easily written in the standard form
(
2.1.1a
)
-
(
2.1.1c
)
.
Both
(
3.4.2b
)

and
(
3.4.2c
)
are linear matrix inequalities like
(
2.1.1b
)
in
variables
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
and
X_{11},X_{12},\dots X_{nn}
;
(
3.4.2b
)
uses “
1\times 1
matrices”
and
(
3.4.2c
)
uses
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrices.
We thus have two inequalities
of the form
(
2.1.1b
)

that can be aggregated into a unique constraint
(
2.1.1b
)
that uses block-diagonal matrices (Prop.
2.1.4
).
As a final side remark, similar programs may even have constraints
like

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-y&\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\\
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}_{0}&A_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\sprod\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{smallmatrix}\right]\leq 0

that contain variables in both factors, because of variable
y
in the left-hand term. However, after developing
the scalar product, the variable
y
simply arises as a term in a final sum
as in
(
2.1.1b
)
.

3.4.3
Unconstrained quadratic programming reduces to SDP programming

We consider an
unconstrained
quadratic program :

\displaystyle\min~~

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}

(3.4.3)

If
A
is not SDP, we can show that this program is unbounded from below. For
this, let us
take any eigenvector
\mathbf{v}
such that
A\mathbf{v}=-\alpha\mathbf{v}
with
\alpha>0
. Consider
the function
f(t)=(t\mathbf{v}^{\top})A(t\mathbf{v})+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{v}=-\alpha t^{2}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+t\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{v}
. This is clearly a strictly concave function in
t
and it goes to
-\infty

when
t
goes to
\infty
.

To solve
(
3.4.3
)
by SDP programming,
one first has to check if
A
is SDP or not. The necessity of this step makes
the approach from Section
3.4.1
unusable here, because we here need a method
that handles at the same time both the case

A\nsucceq{\mathbf{0}}

and

A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.

Let us now show how to address the case
A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
by SDP programming. We first need the following result
which should not be taken from granted (although it is omitted from certain lecture notes).

Proposition 3.4.1
.

If an unconstrained quadratic
program
\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
(i.e., a polynomial of degree 2) is bounded from below, there is
a solution
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
that does reach the minimum value. The polynomial is convex (
A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
) and the
gradient in
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
is
{\mathbf{0}}
.

This does
not hold for polynomials of any
degree or for convex functions
. Indeed,
p(x_{1},x_{2})=(1-x_{1}x_{2})^{2}+x_{1}^{2}
does not reach its
minimum (infimum)
0=\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}p(\frac{1}{m},m)
.
Function
h(x)=e^{x}
is
infinitely differentiable, convex and bounded from below, but there is no
x
with
h^{\prime}(x)=0
that does reach the minimum value.
On the other hand,
it is actually possible to prove than
a convex polynomial (of any
degree) always reaches its minimum value, but the proof of such result is outside the scope of this document.
15
15

15

For a proof, see the response of R. Israel on my question asked on
the on-line forum

https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/2341163/does-a-convex-polynomial-always-reaches-its-minimum-value/
.
The example
(1-x_{1}x_{2})^{2}+x_{1}^{2}
is taken from a response of J.P. McCarthy on

https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/279497/polynomial-px-y-with-inf-mathbbr2-p-0-but-without-any-point-where
.

Proof.

We showed above that the polynomial is bounded only if
A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.

Let us show that if there is
no

\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that cancels the gradient,
the program is
unbounded
. The gradient can be written
\nabla\left(\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\right)=2A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}
, and so,
there is
no

\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
2A\overline{\mathbf{x}}=-\mathbf{b}
. This means that
-\mathbf{b}

(or
\mathbf{b}
) does not belong to the image
(set of linear combinations of the columns)
of
2A
. Using a similar (transposed)
argument as in the first paragraph of the first proof of
Prop.
1.6.4
,
-\mathbf{b}
needs to have the form

-\mathbf{b}=-\mathbf{b}_{\texttt{img}}-\mathbf{b}_{0}
, where

-\mathbf{b}_{\texttt{img}}\in{\texttt{img}}(2A)

and
-\mathbf{b}_{0}\in{\texttt{null}}(2A)
with
\mathbf{b}_{0}\neq{\mathbf{0}}
,
where

{\texttt{img}}(2A)
is the image of
2A
and

{\texttt{null}}(2A)
is the null space of
A
(see the null space definition in

(
A.1.2
)
).

Taking
\mathbf{x}=t\mathbf{b}_{0}
,
we can now define
h(t)=(t\mathbf{b}_{0})^{\top}A(t\mathbf{b}_{0})+(\mathbf{b}_{\texttt{img}}+\mathbf{b}_{0})^{\top}(t\mathbf{b}_{0})=0+t\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{b}_{0}=t|\mathbf{b}_{0}|^{2}
, where we used
\mathbf{b}_{0}\in\texttt{null}(A)\implies A\mathbf{b}_{0}={\mathbf{0}}
. Since
\mathbf{b}_{0}\neq{\mathbf{0}}
, we have

|\mathbf{b}_{0}|\neq 0
, and so,
h(t)
is a non-zero linear function that is clearly
unbounded from below. Since
\mathbf{x}=t\mathbf{b}_{0}
is a perfectly feasible solution of
our unconstrained program, this program is unbounded from below. We proved that
if there is
no
stationary point
\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that cancels the gradient, the program is

unbounded
. This means that a bounded convex quadratic program needs to have some stationary point

\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\nabla\left(\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}\right)_{\overline{\mathbf{x}}}=2A\overline{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{b}={\mathbf{0}}
. For a convex function, the
stationary point needs to be the global minimum, and so,
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
reaches the minimum
value of
\mathbf{x}^{\top}A\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
.
∎

We hereafter consider
(
3.4.3
)
is convex (
A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
).

Proposition 3.4.2
.

The optimum value of
unconstrained convex quadratic program
(
3.4.3
)

is equal to:

\displaystyle t^{*}=\max~~

\displaystyle t

(3.4.4a)

\displaystyle s.t.~

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-t&\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}^{\top}\\
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}&A\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}},

(3.4.4b)

where we use the convention
t^{*}=-\infty
if
(
3.4.4b
)
is infeasible for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
this case being equivalent to the fact that
(
3.4.3
)
is unbounded from below.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that: (i) if
(
3.4.3
)
is unbounded, then

(
3.4.4a
)
-
(
3.4.4b
)
is infeasible and (ii) if
(
3.4.3
)

is bounded then its optimum value is equal to
t^{*}
in
(
3.4.4a
)
-
(
3.4.4b
)
.

Based on Prop
3.4.1
,
the above case (i) can only arise
if either
A
is not SDP so that
(
3.4.4b
)
is clearly infeasible,
or if

(
3.4.3
)

has no stationary point. If there is no stationary point, then there is
no
\overline{\mathbf{x}}

such that

2A\overline{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{b}={\mathbf{0}}
. This means that
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}^{\top}
can not be written
as a linear combination of (the rows of)
A
.
Using Prop.
1.6.4
, we obtain that

(
3.4.4b
)
is infeasible

In the non-degenerate case (ii), Prop.
3.4.1

guarantees that
the quadratic function has a stationary point
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
so that

2A\overline{\mathbf{x}}=-\mathbf{b}
. This stationary point minimizes

(
3.4.3
)
so that the optimum value is

\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}A\overline{\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}=-\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{b}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
. We will prove that
t^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
.

Using Prop.
1.2.4
, the SDP status of

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-t&\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}^{\top}\\
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}&A\end{smallmatrix}\right]
does not change if we add to the first row a linear combination
of the other rows. If we add

\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}&A\end{smallmatrix}\right]
to the first row followed by the same (transposed) operation
on the columns, we obtain the matrix

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}-t+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\mathbf{b}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&A\end{smallmatrix}\right]
. This latter matrix is SDP only if

-t+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\mathbf{b}\geq 0
, which means that the optimum

t^{*}
value is
t^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
, which is also the optimum
value of the unconstrained quadratic function.
∎

Finally, it is interesting that the linear matrix inequality
(
3.4.4b
)

is equivalent to
the following system of
2^{n}
linear inequalities:

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-t&\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}_{J}^{\top}\\
\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}_{J}&A_{J}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\geq 0~\forall J\subseteq[1..n]
, where
\mathbf{b}_{J}
and resp.
A_{J}
are obtained
from
\mathbf{b}
and resp.
A
by selecting rows or columns
J
. When
A\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
,
(
3.4.4a
)
-
(
3.4.4b
)
can actually be written as a
linear program.

3.5
An LP with equality constraints as an SDP program in the dual form

Consider the Linear Program (LP):

\displaystyle\max~

\displaystyle b_{1}x_{1}+b_{2}x_{2}+\dots b_{n}x_{n}

\displaystyle s.t.~

\displaystyle c^{j}_{0}+c^{j}_{1}x_{1}+c^{j}_{2}x_{2}+\dots c^{j}_{n}x_{n}=0~\forall j\in[1..m]

There are two ways of converting this LP into an SDP. The direct method consists of replacing each
equality with two inequalities and of applying Prop.
2.1.4
on the resulting
system of inequalities. We obtain an aggregated SDP programs expressed with aggregated diagonal
matrices.

The second method uses the property

\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\left(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{x}\end{bmatrix}[1~\mathbf{x}^{\top}]\right)=\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{x}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{2}
proved in Lemma
1.3.3.1
,
where
\mathbf{c}^{j}=[c^{j}_{0}~c^{j}_{1}~c^{j}_{2}\dots c^{j}_{n}]^{\top}~\forall j\in[1..m]
. We can thus write:

\mathbf{c}^{j}\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{x}\end{bmatrix}=0~\iff~\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=0.

(3.5.1)

Thus, any feasible solution
\mathbf{x}
of the above LP
can be directly converted to a feasible solution
of the SDP program below by simply setting

X=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}
and performing a sum over
all
j\in[1..m]
.

\displaystyle\max~

\displaystyle b_{1}x_{1}+b_{2}x_{2}+\dots b_{n}x_{n}

\displaystyle s.t.~

\displaystyle\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}=0

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

The last point to prove is that any feasible solution
or

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{smallmatrix}\right]

of the above
SDP program can be associated to a feasible solution
\mathbf{x}
of the LP.
First, we notice that

\left(\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}=0\implies\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}=0~\forall j\in[1..m],

because for each
j\in[1..m]
we have a non-negative
product of two SDP matrices in the left-hand side sum,
i.e.
,

\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{smallmatrix}\right]\geq 0~\forall j\in[1..m]
.
We can now use that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{smallmatrix}\right]\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

is equivalent to
X\succeq\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}
so as to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&X\end{bmatrix}=0

\displaystyle\implies\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}0&{\mathbf{0}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&X-\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}+\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=0

\displaystyle\implies\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=0,

where we used the fact that all involved matrices are SDP and that their scalar products are
non-negative.
We can now apply
(
3.5.1
)
to obtain the implication below, which confirms
\mathbf{x}
is feasible in the initial LP.

\left(\mathbf{c}^{j}{\mathbf{c}^{j}}^{\top}\right)\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathbf{x}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=0\implies\mathbf{c}^{j}\sprod\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{x}\end{bmatrix}=0~\forall j\in[1..m].

PART 2: MORE ADVANCED SDP PROGRAMMING

4
Six equivalent formulations of the Lovász theta number
\vartheta(G)

4.1
A first SDP formulation of the theta number

4.1.1
The primal form
(\vartheta_{G})

We consider a graph
G=([1..n],E)
.
We introduce the Lovász theta number using the following program
based on SDP matrix
\overline{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

\displaystyle[left={(\vartheta_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle t

(4.1.1a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{ii}

\displaystyle=t-1

\displaystyle~~\forall i\in[1..n]

(4.1.1b)

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-1

\displaystyle~~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E

(4.1.1c)

\displaystyle\overline{Z}

\displaystyle\succeq{\mathbf{0}}.

(4.1.1d)

We use the notational convention
\vartheta(G)=OPT(\vartheta_{G})
.
We consider above
(\vartheta_{G})
as a primal SDP program of the form
(
2.1.1a
)
-
(
2.1.1c
)
in which
the variables are
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\overline{z}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
.
The matrix
B
from
(
2.1.1a
)
-
(
2.1.1c
)
contains ones on the diagonal and on
all
positions
(i,j)
corresponding to

\{i,j\}\notin E
.
As a side remark,
using
(
3.2.1a
)
, we observe that
(\vartheta_{G})
returns the maximum eigenvalue of
the matrix
B
described above.

Theorem 4.1.1
.

(
\vartheta(G)\geq\alpha(G)
) The Lovász theta number

\vartheta(G)
is greater than or equal to the maximum stable
\alpha(G)
of
G
.

Proof.

Let us consider the maximum stable
J\in[1..n]
with
|J|=\alpha(G)
. If we restrict
\overline{Z}

from
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
to its minor corresponding to rows
J
and columns
J
, we obtain

tI_{\alpha(G)}-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
, where

\mathds{1}

is a matrix in which all
elements are equal to 1. The fact that
t\geq\alpha(G)
follows
from the next lemma (that needs to hold for
n=\alpha(G)
).
∎

Lemma 4.1.1.1
.

The lowest
z\in\mathbb{R}
for which the
n\times n
matrix below is SDP is
z=n-1
.

A_{n}(z)=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}z&-1&-1&\dots&-1\\
-1&z&-1&\dots&-1\\
-1&-1&z&\dots&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&-1&-1&\dots&z\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{n}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

We provide four proofs so as to
master
multiple proof techniques
and explore a variety of SDP tools
presented in this manuscript.

Proof 1.
We can write
A_{n}(z)=(z+1)-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}
, where

\mathds{1}

is a matrix filled with
n\times n
ones. Using

(
3.2.1a
)
, the minimum value
(z+1)
such that

(z+1)-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
is the maximum eigenvalue of

\mathds{1}

.
But what are the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of

\mathds{1}

? Since
all positions of

{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\mathbf{v}
must be equal for any eigenvector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we obtain that
either (i) the eigenvalue
\lambda_{v}
of
v
is zero or (ii) all elements of

\mathbf{v}
are equal so that
\lambda_{v}=n
. The sought value
z+1
is thus
n
,
leading to
z=n-1
.
∎.

Proof 2.
It is easy to see that the Frobenius
norm of
A_{n}(0)
is
|A_{n}(0)|=\sqrt{\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}A_{n}(0)_{ij}}=\sqrt{(n-1)^{2}}=n-1
.
Using Prop.
1.4.1
, the minimum eigenvalue of
A_{n}(0)
is at least

-|A_{n}(0)|=-(n-1)
. This is enough to guarantee that
A_{n}(n-1)=(n-1)I_{n}+A_{n}(0)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
We show
A_{n}(z)\nsucceq{\mathbf{0}}
for any
z<n-1
by
noticing
[1~1~1\dots 1]A_{n}(z)[1~1~1\dots 1]^{\top}<0
for any
z<n-1
. This proves
that
z=n-1
is
the lowest
z
such that
A_{n}(z)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
∎

Proof 3.
We apply the Gershgorin circle Theorem

A.2.8
to show
A_{n}(n-1)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
. The theorem states that any
eigenvalue
\lambda
of
A_{n}(n-1)
needs to satisfy

\left|\lambda-A_{n}(n-1)_{ii}\right|\leq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}|A_{n}(n-1)_{ij}|
for some

i\in[1..n]
. However, for any
i\in[1..n]
this reduces to

\left|\lambda-(n-1)\right|\leq n-1
, which means
\lambda\geq 0
, leading to

A_{n}(n-1)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
To show
A_{n}(z)\nsucceq{\mathbf{0}}
for any
z<n-1
, use the method from the last two sentences
of above
Proof 2
.
∎

Proof 4.
We proceed by induction. The lemma is clearly true for
n=1
.
For any
n\geq 2
,
we need to have
z>0
because
z=0
would make negative any
2\times 2
minor (use Prop
1.2.5
).
We apply the Schur complement from Prop
1.3.2
. Using the notations
A
,
B
and
C

from Prop
1.3.2
, we write
A_{n}(z)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&B^{\top}\\
B&C\end{smallmatrix}\right]

with
A=z
(which satisfies condition
A\succ{\mathbf{0}}
),

B^{\top}=-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}_{n-1}=\underbrace{[-1~-1~\dots-1]}_{n-1\text{ positions }}
, and
C=A_{n-1}(z)
,
i.e.
,

A_{n-1}(z)
is the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
bottom-right minor. Using the Schur complement,
we obtain
A_{n}(z)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff C-BA^{-1}B^{\top}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff A_{n-1}(z)-\frac{1}{z}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}_{n-1}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}_{n-1}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
. This last
matrix inequality boils down to:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}z-\frac{1}{z}&-1-\frac{1}{z}&\dots&-1-\frac{1}{z}\\
-1-\frac{1}{z}&z-\frac{1}{z}&\dots&-1-\frac{1}{z}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1-\frac{1}{z}&-1-\frac{1}{z}&\dots&z-\frac{1}{z}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{n-1}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle\iff\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\frac{(z+1)(z-1)}{z}&-\frac{z+1}{z}&\dots&-\frac{z+1}{z}\\
-\frac{z+1}{z}&\frac{(z+1)(z-1)}{z}&\dots&-\frac{z+1}{z}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-\frac{z+1}{z}&-\frac{z+1}{z}&\dots&\frac{(z+1)(z-1)}{z}\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{n-1}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

\displaystyle\iff\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}&z-1&-1&\dots&-1\\
&-1&z-1&\dots&-1\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&-1&-1&\dots&z-1\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{n-1}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

We have obtained that
A_{n}(z)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff A_{n-1}(z-1)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
. We can use the
induction hypothesis: the lowest
z-1
such that
A_{n-1}(z-1)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
is
z-1=n-2
,
and so,
z=n-1
is the lowest
z
such that
A_{n}(z)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.∎

Theorem 4.1.2
.

(
\vartheta(G)\leq\chi(\overline{G})
) The Lovász theta
number
\vartheta(G)
is less than or equal to the clique cover number of
G
.
This clique cover number is the chromatic number of the complementary graph

\overline{G}
.

Proof.

Consider a partition
(C_{1},C_{2},\dots C_{k})
of the vertex set

[1..n]
such that each
C_{i}
(with
i\in[1..k]
) is a clique.
Applying Lemma
4.1.1.1
above, we have

\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}k-1&-1&-1&\dots&-1\\
-1&k-1&-1&\dots&-1\\
-1&-1&k-1&\dots&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&-1&-1&\dots&k-1\\
\end{bmatrix}}_{k}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

This means (use the Cholesky factorisation from Prop
1.6.7
or
other decompositions from Corollary
1.7.1
) there exist

k
-dimensional vectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots\mathbf{v}_{k}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{i}=k-1

and
\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{j}=-1~\forall i,j\in[1..k],i\neq j
.

Since
\{C_{1},C_{2},\dots C_{k}\}
cover the vertex set
[1..n]
, all vertices

u\in[1..n]
can be associated to vector
\mathbf{v}^{u}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell(u)}
, where
\ell(u)
is the clique that contains
u

(we have
u\in C_{\ell(u)}
). We actually associate the same vector to all
vertices of a clique.

We now define the matrix
\overline{Z}
by setting

\overline{z}_{ij}=\mathbf{v}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}^{j}
. This is a Gram matrix that is clearly SDP
(use Prop.
A.1.8
). Notice
that: (i)
\overline{z}_{ii}=k-1~\forall i\in[1..n]
and (ii) if

i,j\in[1..n]
do not belong to the same clique, then
\overline{z}_{ij}=-1
,
and so,
\overline{z}_{ij}=-1~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
. Properties
(i) and (ii) are enough to ensure that
\overline{Z}
can be written in
the form
(
4.1.1b
)
-
(
4.1.1c
)
with
t=k
. We have constructed a feasible solution
of
(\vartheta_{G})
from
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
with objective value
k
.
Taking
k=\chi(\overline{G})
, we have

OPT(\vartheta_{G})\leq\chi(\overline{G})
.

This proof is inspired from a related result of a lecture note of Anupam
Gupta.
16
16

16

See (11.3) of the document available, as of 2017, at
http://www.cs.cmu.edu/afs/cs.cmu.edu/academic/class/15859-f11/www/notes/lecture11.pdf
.

∎

Using
\alpha(G)=\omega(\overline{G})
(where

\omega
denotes the maximum clique size), we also obtain the “sandwich” property
as a direct consequence of Theorem
4.1.1
and
Theorem
4.1.2
.

\omega(\overline{G})\leq\vartheta(G)\leq\chi(\overline{G})

(4.1.2)

The following corollary of Lemma
4.1.1.1
can be generally useful, but we do not use it in this
document.

Corollary 4.1.3
.

When
n\geq 2
unitary vectors are as spread out as much as possible, the dot product of any pair
of them is
\frac{-1}{n-1}
.

Proof.

By “spread out as much as possible”, we want

t=\max\{\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{j}:~i,j\in[1..n],~i\neq j\}
to be as low as possible.
Lemma
4.1.1.1
states that
nI_{n}-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}},

where

\mathds{1}

is a matrix with all elements equal to 1.
By dividing all terms by
\frac{1}{n-1}
, we obtain a matrix
T\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
with 1 on the
diagonal and with
\frac{-1}{n-1}
on all non-diagonal positions.
This means that
t\leq\frac{-1}{n-1}
. This
t
value is optimal. If we
decrease any non-diagonal term(s) of
T
, we obtain a matrix

T^{\prime}\nsucceq{\mathbf{0}}
, since
[1~1\dots 1]T^{\prime}[1~1\dots 1]^{\top}<0
.
∎

4.1.2
The dual form of
(\vartheta_{G})

We now introduce the dual program
(D\vartheta_{G})
of
(\vartheta_{G})
from

(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
. Let
D\vartheta(G)=OPT(D\vartheta_{G})
.

\displaystyle[left={(D\vartheta_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\max~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{ii}+\sum_{\{i,j\}\notin E}Y_{ij}={\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod Y

(4.1.3a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\texttt{trace}(Y)=1

(4.1.3b)

\displaystyle Y_{ij}=0~~~\forall\{i,j\}\in E

(4.1.3c)

\displaystyle Y\succeq{\mathbf{0}},

(4.1.3d)

where
(
4.1.3a
)
simplifies to
{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod Y
because
Y_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
by virtue of
(
4.1.3c
)
. Notice that both
(\vartheta_{G})

and
(D\vartheta_{G})
are strictly feasible (take a sufficiently large
t
in

(\vartheta_{G})
and
Y=\frac{1}{n}I_{n}
in
(D\vartheta_{G})
), and so,
we can apply the strong duality Theorem
2.3.5
to state that
the optimum value is reached by both programs.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

The “sandwich” property

\alpha({G})\leq\vartheta(G)\leq\chi(\overline{G})

obtained in
(
4.1.2
)
in the previous Section
4.1.1

can also be proved using the dual
(D\vartheta_{G})
.

Proof of

\alpha({G})\leq\vartheta(G)

Take the largest stable
S
of
G
. Construct matrix
Y^{S}
such that

Y^{S}_{ij}=\frac{1}{|S|}~\forall i,j\in S
and
Y^{S}_{ij}=0
otherwise. It is not hard to
check that
Y^{S}
is a feasible solution of
(D\vartheta_{G})
in

(
4.1.3a
)
-
(
4.1.3d
)
with objective value
|S|
. This is enough
to state
\alpha(G)\leq D\vartheta(G)=\vartheta(G)
.

Proof of

\vartheta(G)\leq\chi(\overline{G})

Consider a clique cover
\{C_{1},~C_{2},\dots C_{k}\}
of
G
.
Without loss of
generality, we can re-order
[1..n]
such that all
C_{i}
(with
i\in[1..k]
)
represent segments of
[1..n]
,
i.e.
,
C_{1}
contains the first
|C_{1}|
elements

\big[1..|C_{1}|\big]
,

C_{2}=\big[|C_{1}|+1..|C_{1}|+|C_{2}|\big]
, etc. We can decompose
Y

of
(D\vartheta(G))
from
(
4.1.3a
)
-
(
4.1.3d
)

into
k^{2}

blocks:

Y=\begin{bmatrix}\pazocal{Y}_{11}&\pazocal{Y}_{12}&\ldots&\pazocal{Y}_{1k}\\
\pazocal{Y}_{21}&\pazocal{Y}_{22}&\ldots&\pazocal{Y}_{2k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\pazocal{Y}_{k1}&\pazocal{Y}_{k2}&\ldots&\pazocal{Y}_{kk}\end{bmatrix},

where block
\pazocal{Y}_{ij}
has size
|C_{i}|\times|C_{j}|
for any
i,j\in[1..k]
.
Notice that
\pazocal{Y}_{ii}
is diagonal because
C_{i}
is a clique (
\forall i\in[1..k]
).
This means
\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{ii}=1
, based on
\texttt{trace}(Y)=1
.
We will show that

{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod Y=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{ij}\leq k.

(4.1.4)

The principal minor of
Y
associated to rows and columns
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
needs to be
SDP. We thus obtain

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\pazocal{Y}_{ii}&\pazocal{Y}_{ij}\\
\pazocal{Y}_{ji}&\pazocal{Y}_{jj}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
Take
\mathbf{x}^{\top}=[\underbrace{1~1\dots 1}_{|C_{i}|}~\underbrace{-1~-1~\dots-1}_{|C_{j}|}]
. Using

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\pazocal{Y}_{ii}&\pazocal{Y}_{ij}\\
\pazocal{Y}_{ji}&\pazocal{Y}_{jj}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\sprod\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}\geq 0
, we obtain:

{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{ij}\leq\frac{{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{i}+{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{j}}{2},~\forall i,j\in[1..k]

Notice this holds (with equality) for terms
i=j\in[1..k]
. We can simply
now obtain
(
4.1.4
)
by applying

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{ij}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{i}+{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{j}}{2}=\frac{2k\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\sprod\pazocal{Y}_{i}}{2}=k.

4.2
A second SDP formulation
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
of the theta number

We start from the following well-known linear program for the maximum
stable
\alpha(G)
.

\displaystyle[left={(\alpha_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\max~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle y_{i}+y_{j}\leq 1~\forall\{i,j\}\in E

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{n}

We now introduce a first SDP relaxation of above
(\alpha_{G})
.

\displaystyle[left={(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\max~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{0i}

(4.2.2a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle y_{00}=1

(4.2.2b)

\displaystyle y_{ij}=0

\displaystyle\forall\{i,j\}\in E

(4.2.2c)

\displaystyle y_{ii}=y_{0i}

\displaystyle\forall i\in[1..n]

(4.2.2d)

\displaystyle Y\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(4.2.2e)

It is not hard to check that
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
is a relaxation of
(\alpha_{G})
.
Take an optimal solution
\mathbf{y}
of
(\alpha_{G})
and construct

Y=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{y}\end{smallmatrix}\right][1~\mathbf{y}^{\top}]
that is
feasible for
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
and has the same objective value as in

(\alpha_{G})
.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

The optimum value
\vartheta^{\prime}(G)
of
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
from

(
4.2.2a
)
-
(
4.2.2e
)
is equal to
the Lovász theta number
i.e., to
the optimum value
\vartheta(G)

of
(\vartheta_{G})
from
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
.

Proof.

We first show that
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
is equivalent to the following
relaxation of
(\alpha_{G})
.

\displaystyle[left={(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\max~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}2y_{0i}-y_{ii}

(4.2.3a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle y_{00}=1

(4.2.3b)

\displaystyle y_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E

(4.2.3c)

\displaystyle Y\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(4.2.3d)

We take an optimal solution
Y
of
(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})

and we will show it satisfies
y_{ii}=y_{0i}~\forall i\in[1..n]
.
Consider any
i\in[1..n]
. If
y_{ii}=0
, then
y_{0i}
needs to be zero as
well. If
y_{ii}\neq 0
, we can multiply row
i
and column
i
of
Y
by

\frac{y_{0i}}{y_{ii}}
to obtain a matrix that is still
feasible for
(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
and whose objective value is greater than
or equal to that of
Y
. This simply follows from

2y_{0i}\frac{y_{0i}}{y_{ii}}-y_{ii}\frac{y^{2}_{0i}}{y^{2}_{ii}}\geq 2y_{0i}-y_{ii}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y_{ii}>0}}{{\iff}}y^{2}_{0i}\geq 2y_{0i}y_{ii}-y^{2}_{ii}\iff(y_{0i}-y_{ii})^{2}\geq 0
.
This proves
OPT(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=OPT(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
. We will show that

OPT(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=OPT(\vartheta_{G})=\vartheta(G)
.

Let us write the dual of
(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
.

\displaystyle[left={(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle t

(4.2.4a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}t&-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}\\
-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}&Z\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}t&-1&-1&-1&\ldots&-1\\
-1&1&z_{12}&z_{13}&\ldots&z_{n1}\\
-1&z_{21}&1&z_{23}&\ldots&z_{n2}\\
-1&z_{31}&z_{32}&1&\ldots&z_{n3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
-1&z_{n1}&z_{n2}&z_{n3}&\ldots&z_{nn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(4.2.4b)

\displaystyle z_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E

(4.2.4c)

Programs
(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
in

(
4.2.3a
)
-
(
4.2.3d
)
and
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})

in
(
4.2.4a
)
-
(
4.2.4c
)
are strictly feasible. For

(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
, it is enough to take
Y=I
.
For
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
, we have

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}n+1&-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}\\
-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}&I_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\succ{\mathbf{0}}
, as certified by applying the Sylvester criterion
(Prop.
1.5.2
) in reversed order (from bottom-right to top-left).
The determinant of the whole matrix is 1: if we add the last
n
rows to
the first one, we obtain a first row with only one non-zero element of value 1 in
the upper-left corner. As such, using the strong duality
Theorem
2.3.5
, we have

OPT(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=OPT(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
and this value
is effectively reached by both programs.

We now reformulate
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
and let us focus on

(
4.2.4b
)
. Using the Schur complement Prop.
1.3.2
, we
have

\begin{bmatrix}t&-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}\\
-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}&Z\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff Z-\frac{1}{t}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff\frac{tZ-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}}{t}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\iff tZ-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}},

where we used several times
t>0
(notice
t=0
would render

(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
infeasible because of the upper-left 2
\times
2
minor of the matrix in
(
4.2.4b
)
). Writing
\overline{Z}=tZ-{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}
, we notice

\overline{z}_{ii}=t-1~\forall i\in[1..n]

(because
z_{ii}=1
in
(
4.2.4b
)
) and
\overline{z}_{ij}=-1~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
(because
z_{ij}=0
in
(
4.2.4c
)
). If
\{i,j\}\in E
, we have
\overline{z}_{ij}=tz_{ij}-1
,
i.e.
,

\overline{z}_{ij}
can actually take any real value, independent from the
other terms of the matrix. Replacing this in
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
, we
obtain that
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
is equivalent
to following program which is exactly
(\vartheta_{G})
from
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
.

\displaystyle[left={(\vartheta_{G})\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{ii}

\displaystyle=t-1

\displaystyle~~\forall i\in[1..n]

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{ij}

\displaystyle=-1

\displaystyle~~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E

\displaystyle\overline{Z}

\displaystyle\succeq{\mathbf{0}}.

All programs presented during the proof have the same optimum,
i.e.
, we proved
that

OPT(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})=OPT(\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=OPT(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=OPT(\vartheta_{G})=\vartheta(G)
. The arguments from this proof are based on a
reversed version of the proof from Section 3.1. of the PhD thesis of
of Nebojša Gvozdenović.
17
17

17

As of 2019, it is available on-line at

http://pure.uva.nl/ws/files/4245957/54393_thesis.pdf
.
∎

4.3
A formulation
\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
of the theta number without SDP matrices

We associate an unit vector
\mathbf{u}^{i}
for each vertex
i\in[1..n]
. We say that
the unit vectors
\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}
constitute an
orthonormal
representation
of

G=([1..n],E)
if and only if
\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{j}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E,~i\neq j
.
We introduce the following function:

\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)=\min\left\{\max_{i\in[1..n]}\frac{1}{(\mathbf{c}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{i})^{2}}:~|\mathbf{c}|=1,~\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}\text{ is an orthonormal representation of }G\right\}

(4.3.1)

Given any
\mathbf{c}
and
\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}
that yield an optimum in
above
(
4.3.1
)
, we can apply a rotation
R
that maps
\mathbf{c}

into unitary vector
R\mathbf{c}=[1~0~0~0\dots 0]^{\top}
and
\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}

into
\{\mathbf{u}^{\prime 1},\mathbf{u}^{\prime 2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{\prime n}\}
. A rotation is always represented by
a matrix
R
that is unitary and orthonormal,
18
18

18

We actually still need to prove there exists a rotation of the space
that maps the unitary vector
\mathbf{c}
to
\overline{\mathbf{c}}=[1~0~0~0\dots 0]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
This task would be very simple if
\mathbf{c}
had the form

\mathbf{c}=[c_{1}~c_{2}~0~0~0\dots 0]^{\top}
,
i.e.
, it would be enough to
apply a rotation on the (space of) the first two coordinates and keep
all remaining
k-2
coordinates fixed.
We will construct a basis so as to make the representation
of
\mathbf{c}
in this basis always have the above form.
Let us take an orthonormal
basis
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
(with
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\overline{\mathbf{c}}
) of the sub-space spanned
by
\mathbf{c}
and
\overline{\mathbf{c}}
and complete it to a full
orthonormal basis by adding
\mathbf{v}_{3},~\mathbf{v}_{4}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k}
.
Writing
V=[\mathbf{v}_{1}~\mathbf{v}_{2}~\mathbf{v}_{3}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k}]
, any vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
can be
represented in the
new basis as
\mathbf{v}=V\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
,
i.e.
,

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=V^{-1}\mathbf{v}
is the expression of
\mathbf{v}
in the new basis.
Notice
\mathbf{c}
and
\overline{\mathbf{c}}
can be written as linear combinations of
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and

\mathbf{v}_{2}
, and so, their representations
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
in the new basis
only use the first two coordinates.
After checking that
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
are unitary,
we can define
\alpha=\arccos\mathbf{c}^{\prime}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}
.
The matrix
R_{2}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\cos\alpha&sin\alpha\\
-\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right]
rotates by
\alpha

any 2-dimensional point, and so, it maps
\overline{\mathbf{c}}
to
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
in the space of the
first
two coordinates (or
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
to
\overline{\mathbf{c}}
, case in which we replace
\alpha
by

-\alpha
).
We extend
R_{2}
to
R_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
by putting an
one on each new diagonal position
(i,i)
with
i>2
and zeros on all other
positions
(i,j)
with
i>2
or
j>2
.
R_{k}
rotates the first two coordinates
of any vector
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and leaves untouched the rest of values
v^{\prime}_{3},~v^{\prime}_{3}\dots v^{\prime}_{k}
.
Let us calculate
VR_{k}V^{\top}\mathbf{c}=VR_{k}V^{\top}V\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=VR_{k}\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=V\overline{\mathbf{c}}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathbf{c}}
.
Since
VR_{k}V^{\top}
is orthonormal, it is a rotation matrix that
performs the desired rotation.

and so, does not change scalar products or norms. Indeed, if
R^{\top}R=I
, for any two vectors

\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
we have
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}^{\top}R^{\top}R\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=(R\mathbf{v})^{\top}(R\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
,
and so, the mapping
\mathbf{v}\to R\mathbf{v}
does not change scalar products or norms.
This means that
[1~0~0~0\dots 0]^{\top}
and
\{\mathbf{u}^{\prime 1},\mathbf{u}^{\prime 2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{\prime n}\}
is
also an optimal solution for
(
4.3.1
)
. As such, we can simplify

(
4.3.1
)
to:

\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)=\min\left\{\max_{i\in[1..n]}\left(\frac{1}{{u_{1}^{i}}}\right)^{2}:~\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}\text{ is an orthonormal representation of }G\right\}

(4.3.2)

Theorem 4.3.1
.

\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)=\vartheta(G)

Proof.

We will first show
\vartheta(G)\leq\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
and then
\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\vartheta(G)
.

\vartheta(G)\leq\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)

We will start from an optimal orthonormal representation of
G
and we will construct a feasible
solution of

(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
from
(
4.2.4a
)
-
(
4.2.4c
)
that we proved
(Theorem
4.2.1
) to reach the optimum
\vartheta(G)
.
Without loss of generality, we can consider
|u^{1}_{1}|\leq|u^{i}_{1}|~\forall i\in[1..n]
, and
so,
(
4.3.2
)
states that
\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)=\left(\frac{1}{u^{1}_{1}}\right)^{2}
. Let us
construct vectors
\mathbf{v}^{0},~\mathbf{v}^{1},~\mathbf{v}^{2},\dots\mathbf{v}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{v}^{0}=[-\frac{1}{u^{1}_{1}}~0~0~0\dots 0]^{\top}
and for all
i\in[1..n]
we set
\mathbf{v}^{i}=\frac{u^{1}_{1}}{u^{i}_{1}}\mathbf{u}^{i}
. We construct the Gram matrix
Z^{\prime}

of these vectors such that
Z^{\prime}_{ij}=\mathbf{v}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}^{j}~\forall i,j\in[0..n]
. We have
Z^{\prime}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
using Prop.
A.1.8
,
Z^{\prime}_{00}=\left(\frac{1}{u^{1}_{1}}\right)^{2}=\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
, and
Z^{\prime}_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
, using

\mathbf{v}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}^{j}=\frac{u^{1}_{1}}{u^{i}_{1}}\frac{u^{1}_{1}}{u^{j}_{1}}\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{j}
and the fact that

\mathbf{u}^{1},~\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}
are a orthonormal representation of
G
. We also have

Z^{\prime}_{0i}=-1
, because
v^{i}_{1}=u^{1}_{1}~\forall i\in[1..n]
.

The diagonal elements are
Z^{\prime}_{ii}=\left(\frac{u^{1}_{1}}{u^{i}_{1}}\right)^{2}\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{i}\leq 1~\forall i\in[1..n]
, where we used
|u^{1}_{1}|\leq|u^{i}_{1}|
and the fact that

\mathbf{u}_{1},~\mathbf{u}_{2},~\mathbf{u}_{3},\dots\mathbf{u}_{n}
are unitary. We transform
Z^{\prime}
into a matrix
Z
by only increasing the
diagonal elements up to
Z_{ii}=1~\forall i\in[1..n]
;
Z
remains SDP as the sum of
Z

and a positive diagonal matrix. This matrix
Z
is a feasible solution in

(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
in
(
4.2.4a
)
-
(
4.2.4c
)

with objective value
Z^{\prime}_{00}=\left(\frac{1}{u^{1}_{1}}\right)^{2}=\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
.
Since
OPT(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})=\vartheta(G)
as shown in the proof of
Theorem
4.2.1
, we have
\vartheta(G)\leq\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
.

\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\vartheta(G)

We start from
(\vartheta_{G})
in
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
and
we will construct an orthogonal representation of value
\vartheta(G)
.
Notice
the optimal solution
\overline{Z}
of
(
4.1.1a
)
-
(
4.1.1d
)
verifies
\overline{Z}_{ii}=\vartheta(G)-1
and

\overline{Z}_{ij}=-1~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
. Using the Cholesky factorisation
from
Prop.
1.6.7

(or the eigenvalue or the square root factorization as in Corollary
1.7.1
),
there exist
\mathbf{v}_{1},~\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots\mathbf{v}_{n}
such that

\overline{Z}_{ij}=\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{j}
. We construct the representation

\mathbf{u}_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\vartheta(G)}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{v}_{i}\end{bmatrix}~\forall i\in[1..n]
. We verify the following:

–

|\mathbf{u}_{i}|^{2}=\frac{1}{\vartheta(G)}\left(1+\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{i}\right)=\frac{\vartheta(G)}{\vartheta(G)}=1~\forall i\in[1..n]
,
i.e.
,
the representation is unitary;

–

for
\{i,j\}\notin E
, we have
\mathbf{u}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}_{j}=\frac{1}{\vartheta(G)}\left(1+\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}_{j}\right)=0
,
i.e.
,
the representation is orthonormal;

–

the value of the representation in
(
4.3.2
)
is

\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\vartheta(G)}}}\right)^{2}=\vartheta(G)

because
u^{i}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\vartheta(G)}}~\forall i\in[1..n]
.

This shows
\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\vartheta(G)
, which finishes the proof.

The proof of
\vartheta(G)\leq\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)
is personal. The proof of
\vartheta^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\vartheta(G)
is adapted from the proof of “
\vartheta_{1}(G,w)\leq\vartheta_{2}(G,w)
’’ from
Section 6 of the survey article ‘‘The sandwich theorem’’ of Donald Knuth.
19
19

19

Published in the
Electronic Journal of Combinatorics

in 1994 (1), available at

http://www.combinatorics.org/ojs/index.php/eljc/article/view/v1i1a1/pdf
.

∎

4.4
A fourth formulation
\vartheta^{\ell}(G)
of the theta number

We consider an orthonormal representation
\mathbf{u}^{1},~\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}
of
\overline{G}
,
i.e.
, we have

\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{j}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
. The
leaning
of this representation is

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{e}_{1}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{i})^{2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{u}^{i}_{1})^{2}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{1}=[1~\underbrace{0~0\dots 0}_{n-1}]^{\top}
.
One can think of this
leaning
term in the sense that any vector is leaning somehow
on (casts its shadow on) the first dimension. We introduce:

\vartheta^{\ell}(G)=\max\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{e}_{1}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{i})^{2}:~\{\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}\}\text{ is an orthonormal representation of }\overline{G}\right\}

(4.4.1)

Proposition 4.4.1
.

\vartheta^{\ell}(G)=\vartheta(G)

Proof.

We will show that generating an orthonormal representation of
\overline{G}
in

(
4.4.1
)
is equivalent
to generating a feasible solution of
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
in
(
4.2.2a
)
-
(
4.2.2e
)
with the same objective
value. We start by noticing that generating a feasible solution
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times(n+1)}
of
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
is equivalent to generating
n+1

vectors
\mathbf{c},\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{1},\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{2},\dots\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{n}
such that
Y_{ij}=\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{j}~\forall i,j\in[1..n]
and

Y_{0i}=Y_{i0}=\mathbf{c}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]
. Given constraints

(
4.2.2b
)
-
(
4.2.2d
)
, these vectors need to satisfy:
\mathbf{c}\sprod\mathbf{c}=Y_{00}=1
,

\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{j}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
, and

\mathbf{c}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}=\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{i}=d^{2}_{i},~\forall i\in[1..n]
.
The objective value of
Y
in
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
is
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}^{2}
.

Since there exists an orthonormal rotation matrix
R
that maps
\mathbf{c}
to
R\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{e}_{1}
and leaves unchanged scalar products (see
Footnote 17, p.
18
), generating vectors

\mathbf{c},\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{1},\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{2},\dots\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{n}
with above properties
is equivalent to generating vectors

R\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{e}_{1},R\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{1}={\mathbf{v}}^{1},R\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{2}={\mathbf{v}}^{2},\dots R\overline{\mathbf{v}}^{n}={\mathbf{v}}^{n}
with the same pairwise scalar products,
i.e.
,
such that

{\mathbf{v}}^{i}\sprod{\mathbf{v}}^{j}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
and

\mathbf{e}\sprod{\mathbf{v}}^{i}={\mathbf{v}}^{i}\sprod{\mathbf{v}}^{i}=d^{2}_{i},~\forall i\in[1..n]
because
RR^{\top}=I
.
Remark
v^{i}_{1}=d^{2}_{i}
.

Generating vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{1},\mathbf{v}^{2},\dots\mathbf{v}^{n}
with above properties is equivalent to
generating vectors
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{u}^{1},\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}
, where
\mathbf{u}^{i}=\frac{1}{d_{i}}\mathbf{v}^{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]

(if
d_{i}=0
, we have
\mathbf{v}^{i}={\mathbf{0}}
and we set

\mathbf{u}^{i}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{v}^{i}\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathbf{0}}\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right]
and extend all
other
\mathbf{u}^{j}~\forall j\in[1..n]-\{i\}

with a 0 element).
One can check that
|\mathbf{u}^{i}|^{2}=\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{i}=\frac{1}{d^{2}_{i}}\mathbf{v}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}^{i}=1~\forall i\in[1..n]
and

\mathbf{u}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{j}=\frac{1}{d_{i}}\frac{1}{d_{j}}\mathbf{v}^{i}\sprod\mathbf{v}^{j}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
.
This means that
\mathbf{u}^{1},~\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}
is an orthonormal representation of
\overline{G}
such
that
u^{i}_{1}=d_{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]
. The leaning of
\mathbf{u}^{1},~\mathbf{u}^{2},\dots\mathbf{u}^{n}
in
(
4.4.1
)
is
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}^{2}
,
i.e.
,
the same as the objective value of feasible matrix
Y
in
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})
in
(
4.2.2a
)
-
(
4.2.2e
)
.
This confirms
\vartheta^{\ell}(G)=OPT(\vartheta^{\prime}_{G})=\vartheta(G)
.
∎

4.5
Two formulations of the theta number using maximum eigenvalues

4.5.1
A formulation only using the maximum eigenvalue

Let us introduce:

\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)=\max\big\{{\lambda_{\max}}(Z):~Z\succeq{\mathbf{0}},~Z_{ii}=1~\forall i\in[1..n],~z_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E\big\},

(4.5.1)

where
{\lambda_{\max}}(Z)
is the maximum eigenvalue of
Z
.

Theorem 4.5.1
.

\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)=\vartheta(G)

Proof.

Using Lemma
1.2.2.1
, the maximum eigenvalue of

Z
can be computed as
{\lambda_{\max}}(Z)=\max\limits_{|\mathbf{x}|=1}Z\sprod(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top})
.
As such,
(
4.5.1
)
is equivalent to:

\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)=\max\big\{Z\sprod(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}):~Z\succeq{\mathbf{0}},~Z_{ii}=1~\forall i\in[1..n],~Z_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E,~|\mathbf{x}|=1\big\}

(4.5.2)

We show that any feasible solution
(Z,\mathbf{x})
of above program can be mapped to a feasible solution

Y
of
(D\vartheta_{G})
from
(
4.1.3a
)
-
(
4.1.3d
)
. We showed
in Section
4.1.2
that
\vartheta(G)=OPT(D\vartheta_{G})
.
For the reader’s convenience, we recall below the definition of this program.

D\vartheta(G)=\max\left\{Y\sprod{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}:~Y\succeq{\mathbf{0}},~Y_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E,~\texttt{trace}(Y)=1\right\}

(4.5.3)

Consider matrix
Y
obtained by multiplying each row and column
i
of
Z
with
x_{i}
. In
other words,
Y_{ij}=Z_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
. It is not hard to check that
Y
reaches in
(
4.5.3
)

the same objective value
Y\sprod{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}=\sum\limits_{i,j\in[1..n]}Z_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}=Z\sprod(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top})
. Furthermore,
Y
satisfies all constraints of
(
4.5.3
)
,
i.e.
,

\texttt{trace}(Y)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x^{2}_{i}=|\mathbf{x}|^{2}=1
.

By reversing the above transformation, it is quite straightforward to prove the converse: any feasible solution
Y
of
(
4.5.3
)
can be
mapped to a feasible solution
(Z,\mathbf{x})
of
(
4.5.2
)
. For this, we take

x_{i}=\sqrt{Y_{ii}}~\forall i\in[1..n]
. We obtain
Z
by dividing each row and column
i

of
Y
with
x_{i}
for all
i
such that
x_{i}>0
. More exactly, we obtain

Z_{ij}=\frac{Y_{ij}}{x_{i}x_{j}}~\forall i,j\in[1..n],~x_{i}>0,x_{j}>0
. If
x_{i}=0
, we set

Z_{ii}=1
and
Z_{ij}=0~\forall j\in[1..n]-\{i\}
. It is not hard to check that

(Z,\mathbf{x})
satisfies the constraints of
(
4.5.2
)
. For instance, we have

|\mathbf{x}|^{2}=\texttt{trace}(Y)=1
and
Z_{ii}=\frac{Y_{ii}}{\sqrt{Y_{ii}}^{2}}=1
.
One can also check
Y\sprod{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}=\sum\limits_{i,j\in[1..n]}Y_{ij}=\sum\limits_{i,j\in[1..n]}Z_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}=Z\sprod(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top})
,
where we used
Y_{ij}=Z_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}
that holds even if
x_{i}=0
or
x_{j}=0
.

This proves
(
4.5.2
)
and
(
4.5.3
)
are equivalent,
which means
\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)=D\vartheta(G)=\vartheta(G)
.
∎

4.5.2
A formulation using the maximum and the minimum eigenvalue

Let us note
{\lambda_{\min}}(X)
and
{\lambda_{\max}}(X)
the minimum and resp. the maximum
eigenvalue of
X
. We introduce

\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)=\max\big\{1-\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}:~X_{ii}=0~\forall i\in[1..n],~X_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E\big\},

(4.5.4)

where we use the convention
\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}=0
if

{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}={{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}=0
.
Since the only SDP matrix with zeros on the diagonal is
{\mathbf{0}}
,
any feasible
X\neq{\mathbf{0}}
in
above
(
4.5.4
)
verifies
X\nsucceq{\mathbf{0}}
and
X\npreceq{\mathbf{0}}
, and so,

{\lambda_{\max}}(X)>0>{\lambda_{\min}}(X)
. Any
X\neq{\mathbf{0}}
yields a value

1-\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}>1
in
(
4.5.4
)
.
If
X={\mathbf{0}}
is the optimal solution of
(
4.5.4
)
, then
G
must be a clique,
so that constraints
X_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
force every non-diagonal
element of
X
to be zero. In this case, we have

\alpha(G)=\vartheta(G)=\chi(\overline{G})=\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)=1
.

Theorem 4.5.2
.

\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)=\vartheta(G)

Proof.

We take the optimal
X
in
(
4.5.4
)
and we construct a feasible solution
Z
of

(
4.5.1
)
with the same objective value.
As stated above,

X={\mathbf{0}}

leads to

\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)=\vartheta(G)=1

and any
X\neq{\mathbf{0}}

satisfies

{\lambda_{\min}}(X)<0
.
Considering

{\lambda_{\min}}(X)<0
,
we can construct
X^{\prime}=\frac{X}{|{\lambda_{\min}}(X)|}
and notice

\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}=\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X^{\prime})}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X^{\prime})}=-{{\lambda_{\max}}(X^{\prime})}
. This means
X^{\prime}
is also optimal in
(
4.5.4
)
and
has
{\lambda_{\min}}(X)=-1
, more exactly
X^{\prime}
lead
(
4.5.4
)
to objective value

1+{\lambda_{\max}}(X^{\prime})
. Consider now
Z=X^{\prime}+I
and notice
{\lambda_{\min}}(Z)=0
, and so,

Z\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
. One can easily check
Z
is feasible in
(
4.5.1
)
and has
objective value
{\lambda_{\max}}(X^{\prime}+I)={\lambda_{\max}}(X^{\prime})+1
,
i.e.
, the same as the
objective value of
X
or
X^{\prime}
in
(
4.5.4
)
. This shows
\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)\leq\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)=\vartheta(G)
.

We still have to prove
\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)\leq\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)
. We now attempt to reverse
the above process. Let
Z
be the optimal solution
of
(
4.5.1
)
and
take
X=Z-I
. Notice
X
is feasible in
(
4.5.4
)
and that

\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)={\lambda_{\max}}(Z-I+I)=1+{\lambda_{\max}}(X)=1+\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{1}\leq 1-\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)},

(4.5.5)

where we used

0>{\lambda_{\min}}(X)\geq-1
that we prove now.
In fact, we already left aside
the particular case
{\lambda_{\min}}(X)=0
because
that would imply
X\succeq 0
which would further lead
(by applying
\texttt{diag}(X)={\mathbf{0}}
) to
X=0
, which means
the optimal
Z
is
Z=X+I=I
and the graph is a clique.
We can not have
{\lambda_{\min}}(X)>0
because
that would imply
X\succ{\mathbf{0}}
, impossible when

\texttt{diag}(X)={\mathbf{0}}
.
We now notice

Z=X+I\succeq{\mathbf{0}}\implies{\lambda_{\min}}(X+I)\geq 0\implies{\lambda_{\min}}(X)\geq-1
. This shows

0>{\lambda_{\min}}(X)\geq-1
, confirming
the last inequality of
(
4.5.5
)
. This finishes
the proof because
(
4.5.5
)

simplifies to

\vartheta^{\lambda_{\max}}(G)\leq 1-\frac{{\lambda_{\max}}(X)}{{\lambda_{\min}}(X)}\leq\vartheta^{\lambda}(G)
.
∎

4.6
The theta number
\vartheta(G)
is bounded by the fractional chromatic number

\chi^{*}(\overline{G})
of
\overline{G}

4.6.1
The fractional chromatic number

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the set of cliques of
G
. In the primal-dual programs below we introduce the
most standard formulation of the fractional chromatic number of
\overline{G}
. It is not hard to see that
the standard chromatic number
\chi(\overline{G})
is an upper bound for these programs, by
taking
\lambda_{C_{i}}=1~\forall i\in[1..\chi(\overline{G})]
,
where
\{C_{1},~C_{2},\dots C_{\chi(\overline{G})}\}
is the
optimal coloring of
\overline{G}
(clique covering of
G
).

(\hat{\chi}^{*}_{\overline{G}})\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\min&\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}&\\
s.t.&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathscr{C}\\
C\ni i\end{subarray}}\lambda_{C}\geq 1&\forall i\in[1..n]\\
&\lambda_{C}\geq 0&\forall C\in\mathscr{C}\end{array}\right.

(D\hat{\chi}^{*}_{\overline{G}})\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\max&\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}&\\
s.t.&\sum\limits_{i\in C}x_{i}\leq 1&\forall C\in\mathscr{C}\\
&x_{i}\geq 0&\forall i\in[1..n]\end{array}\right.

These primal-dual programs can be modified as follows. Let us drop the non-negativity constraint

\mathbf{x}\geq{\mathbf{0}}
in the dual
(D\hat{\chi}^{*}_{\overline{G}})
.
We prove by contradiction that the resulting program has only non-negative
optimal solutions.
Assume there is an optimal solution
\mathbf{x}
such that
x_{j}<0
for some
j\in[1..n]
.
We show that by increasing
x_{j}
to
0
,
\mathbf{x}
remains feasible. For this, take
any clique
C\ni j
;
since

C-\{j\}
is naturally a clique and
\mathbf{x}
is feasible, we have

\sum_{i\in C-\{j\}}x_{i}\leq 1
. If we now increase
x_{j}
to
0
, the new solution
satisfies

\sum_{i\in C}x_{i}=\sum_{i\in C-\{j\}}x_{i}\leq 1
.
This new solution is still feasible and has a higher objective value, and so,
we obtained a contradiction. The assumption
x_{j}<0
was false.
The above programs are thus equivalent to the next ones,
i.e.
,

OPT(\hat{\chi}^{*}_{\overline{G}})=OPT(D\hat{\chi}^{*}_{\overline{G}})=OPT(\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})=OPT(D\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})=\chi^{*}(\overline{G})

(\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\min&\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}&\\
s.t.&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathscr{C}\\
C\ni i\end{subarray}}\lambda_{C}=1&\forall i\in[1..n]\\
&\lambda_{C}\geq 0&\forall C\in\mathscr{C}\end{array}\right.

(4.6.1a)

(D\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\max&\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}&\\
s.t.&\sum\limits_{i\in C}x_{i}\leq 1&\forall C\in\mathscr{C}\\
&x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}&\forall i\in[1..n]\end{array}\right.

(4.6.1b)

4.6.2
A hierarchy of SDP programs sandwiched between
\vartheta(G)
and
\chi^{*}(\overline{G})

We will use the
(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G})
formulation of
\vartheta(G)
from

(
4.2.4a
)
-
(
4.2.4c
)
. For the reader’s convenience, we repeat
the definition of this program, multiplying by
-1
the first row and column of the
SDP matrix (this does not change its SDP status).

\displaystyle[left={\left(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G}\right)\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle t

(4.6.2a)

\displaystyle s.t.~~

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}t&{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}\\
{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}&Z\end{bmatrix}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}

(4.6.2b)

\displaystyle z_{ii}=1

\displaystyle\forall i\in[1..n]

(4.6.2c)

\displaystyle z_{ij}=0

\displaystyle\forall\{i,j\}\notin E

(4.6.2d)

Notice that
(\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})
in
(
4.6.1a
)
is defined using variables indexed by a
subset of the power set
\mathbb{P}([1..n])
of the vertex set
[1..n]
. This gives us some intuitions
that we might need programs with variables indexed by certain subsets of
[1..n]
. Let us introduce

\mathbb{P}_{r}([1..n])=\left\{S\subseteq[1..n]:~|S|\leq r\right\}~\forall r\in[1..n]
. Given a vector

\mathbf{y}
indexed by all
I\in\mathbb{P}_{2r}([1..n])
, we can write
\mathbf{y}=(y_{I})_{I\in\mathbb{P}_{2r}([1..n])}
. Let us now
introduce a matrix
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})
with rows and columns indexed by
\mathbb{P}_{r}([1..n])
such that

M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{I,J}=\mathbf{y}_{I\cup J}
. We can also compactly write:

M_{r}(\mathbf{y})=(y_{I\cup J})_{I,J\in\mathbb{P}_{r}([1..n])}.

(4.6.3)

We now introduce the following program:

\psi^{r}(G)=\big\{\min y_{\emptyset}:~M_{r}(\mathbf{y})\succeq{\mathbf{0}},~y_{\{i\}}=1~\forall i\in[1..n],~y_{\{i,j\}}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E\big\},

(4.6.4)

where one could notice that
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{I,I}=M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{\emptyset,I}~\forall I\in\mathbb{P}_{r}([1..n])
, in
particular
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{\{i\},\{i\}}=M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{\emptyset,\{i\}}=y_{\{i\}}=1~\forall i\in[1..n]
.

Theorem 4.6.1
.

\vartheta(G)=\psi^{1}(G)\leq\psi^{2}(G)\leq\dots\leq\psi^{\omega(G)}(G)\leq\chi^{*}(\overline{G})
.

Proof.

We first show that

(
4.6.4
)
with
r=1

is equivalent to

\left(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G}\right)
in

(
4.6.2a
)
-
(
4.6.2d
)
.
It is actually enough to carefully “decode” all notations to see that
the two programs are simply
identical up to a notational
translation.
With this goal, we can replace

M_{1}(\mathbf{y})_{\emptyset,\emptyset}=y_{\emptyset}
with
t
,

M_{1}(\mathbf{y})_{\{i\},\{j\}}=y_{\{i,j\}}=0

with
z_{ij}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
,
and
M_{1}(\mathbf{y})_{\{i\},\{j\}}=y_{\{i,j\}}
with
z_{ij}~\forall\{i,j\}\in E
;

M_{\emptyset,\{i\}}=y_{\{i\}}=1
for
i\in[1..n]
is simply translated into
the vectors

\mathds{1}

and
{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathds{1}$}}^{\top}

that border
Z
in
(
4.6.2b
)
.
One can check that we have just mapped one program into another, which guarantees that
\vartheta(G)=OPT\left(D\widetilde{\vartheta^{\prime}}_{G}\right)=\psi^{1}(G)
.

We now show that
\psi^{r}(G)\leq\psi^{r+1}(G)~\forall r\in[1..n]
.
Notice it is not possible to
border a solution

M_{r}(\mathbf{y})
of
\psi^{r}(G)

with zeros
to obtain a solution

M_{r+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
of
\psi^{r+1}(G)
, because
certain elements of

M_{r+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

are inherited from

M_{r}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
,
e.g.
, a proper bordering imposes
M_{r+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})_{\emptyset,[1..r+1]}=M_{r}(\mathbf{y})_{\{1\},[1..r]}
.
Take the
solution
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
that
achieves the optimum value
\psi^{r+1}(G)
.
The key is to notice

M_{r}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
is a principal minor of
M_{r+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
, and so,
M_{r}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.
By removing from
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
all indices
S\in\mathbb{P}_{2r+2}[1..n]-\mathbb{P}_{2r}[1..n]
, we obtain a vector
\mathbf{y}

indexed by
\mathbb{P}_{2r}[1..n]
that generates a feasible solution

M_{r}(\mathbf{y})
of
\psi^{r}(G)
.
Indeed,
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
because
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})=M_{r}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
is a principal minor
of
M_{r+1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
; all other constraints in
(
4.6.4
)
concern the values
of
\mathbf{y}
on sets of
\mathbb{P}_{2}([1..n])
that are inherited from
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
. The objective value is the same,

i.e.
,
y_{\emptyset}=y^{\prime}_{\emptyset}
. This is enough to state
\psi^{r}(G)\leq\psi^{r+1}(G)
, because

(
4.6.4
)
has a minimizing objective. The value
\psi^{r}(G)
could be even strictly less
that
y_{\emptyset}
because
\psi^{r}(G)
is obtained with a program
(
4.6.4
)
with fewer
constraints than
\psi^{r+1}(G)
,
i.e.
, it involves smaller SDP matrices
M_{r}(\mathbf{y})
. In other words, there might
exist some
\overline{\mathbf{y}}
indexed by
\mathbb{P}_{2r}([1..n])
that achieves a lower value
\psi^{r}(G)
and that can not
be extended to some feasible
\overline{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}
indexed by
\mathbb{P}_{2i+2}([1..n])
.

We finally show
\psi^{\omega(G)}(G)\leq\chi^{*}(\overline{G})
. We take the optimum solution

\lambda
of
(\chi^{*}_{\overline{G}})
from
(
4.6.1a
)
and construct a feasible
solution with the same objective value in
(
4.6.4
)
with

r=\omega(G)=\mathbb{P}_{\omega(G)}([1..n])
. Take any
clique
C\in\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\omega(G)}([1..n])
and construct
\mathbf{y}^{C}
indexed by
\mathbb{P}_{2\omega(G)}([1..n])
such that

y^{C}_{S}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }S\subseteq C\\
0&\text{if }S\nsubseteq C\\
\end{cases}

(4.6.5)

One only needs to decode notations to see that

M_{\omega(G)}(\mathbf{y}^{C})=\overline{\mathbf{y}^{C}}~\overline{\mathbf{y}^{C}}^{\top}\in\{0,1\}^{P_{\omega(G)}([1..n])\times P_{\omega(G)}([1..n])}
, where

\overline{\mathbf{y}^{C}}
is a reduced version of
\mathbf{y}^{C}
that contains only

\mathbb{P}_{\omega(G)}([1..n])
elements
\overline{y^{C}_{S}}=y^{C}_{S}
with
S\in\mathbb{P}_{\omega(G)}([1..n])
. Indeed, if
M_{\omega(G)}(\mathbf{y}^{C})_{I,J}=0
, then
I\cup J\nsubseteq C
using

(
4.6.5
)
, and so, we have
I\nsubseteq C
or
J\nsubseteq C
, which means that
y^{C}_{I}=0
or

y^{C}_{J}=0
. Also, if
M_{\omega(G)}(\mathbf{y}^{C})_{I,J}=1
, then we have
I\cup J\subseteq C
by virtue of

(
4.6.5
)
, which means that
I,J\subseteq C
, and so,
y^{C}_{I}=y^{C}_{J}=1
. This confirms that

M_{\omega(G)}(\mathbf{y}^{C})=\overline{\mathbf{y}^{C}}~\overline{\mathbf{y}^{C}}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.

By applying
(
4.6.3
)
on
\mathbf{y}=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}\mathbf{y}^{C}
we obtain

M_{\omega(G)}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)=M_{\omega(G)}\left(\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}\mathbf{y}^{C}\right)=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}M_{\omega(G)}\left(\mathbf{y}^{C}\right)
.
As a sum of PSD matrices
M_{\omega(G)}(\mathbf{y}^{C})
resp. multiplied by positive scalars
\lambda_{C}
,
the matrix
M_{\omega(G)}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)
is SDP. We now check the two
non-SDP constraints of
(
4.6.4
)
.
First, we have

y_{\{i\}}=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}\mathbf{y}^{C}_{\{i\}}=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C},C\ni i}\lambda_{C}=1~\forall i\in[1..n]
, using
(
4.6.1a
)
.
Secondly,
y_{\{i,j\}}=0~\forall\{i,j\}\notin E
also holds because the non-edge
\{i,j\}

belongs to no clique. We have just constructed a feasible solution
\mathbf{y}
in
(
4.6.4
)
for

r=\omega(G)
with objective value

y_{\emptyset}=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}\mathbf{y}^{C}_{\emptyset}=\sum\limits_{C\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda_{C}=\chi^{*}(\overline{G})
.
This is enough to conclude
\psi^{\omega(G)}(G)\leq\chi^{*}(\overline{G})
.

Parts of this proof are a simplification of the proof of Theorem 3.1. from
the article “The operator
\Psi
for the chromatic number of a graph’’
of Nebojša Gvozdenović and Monique Laurent.
20
20

20

Published in
SIAM Journal on Optimization
in 2008, vol 19(2), pp. 572–591,
available on-line as of 2017 at

http://homepages.cwi.nl/~monique/files/SIOPTGL1.pdf
.

∎

5
A taste of copositive optimization and sum of squares hierarchies

5.1
Introducing the completely positive and the copositive cones

Let us try to produce better relaxations and reformulations
by replacing the cone
S_{n}^{+}
of SDP matrices with a smaller cone.
For this, we introduce the cone of
completely positive matrices
:

\displaystyle C^{n*}

\displaystyle=\left\{X\in S_{n}:~X=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{y}_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\top}\text{ with }\mathbf{y}_{i}\geq{\mathbf{0}}~\forall i\in[1..k]\right\}

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle=\texttt{conv}\left\{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}:\mathbf{y}\geq{\mathbf{0}}~\right\},

(5.1.2)

where
S_{n}\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the set of real symmetric matrices
and the operator
\texttt{conv}(...)
produces all convex combinations of the elements from the set given as argument.
It is clear that any
X
from
(
5.1.1
)
can be written as a convex combination of rank-1 matrices
as in
(
5.1.2
)
.
For this, it is enough to write
X=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{y}_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{k}\left(\sqrt{k}\mathbf{y}_{i}\right)\left(\sqrt{k}\mathbf{y}_{i}\right)^{\top}
.
Any
X\in C^{n*}

can be written
X=YY^{\top}
with
Y=[\mathbf{y}_{1}~\mathbf{y}_{2}~\dots\mathbf{y}_{k}]
, and so,
it is clear that
X
is also SDP using Prop.
A.1.8
.
The difference between a completely positive matrix
X
and an SDP one is that
above
Y
needs to be non-negative. Any SDP matrix
\widetilde{X}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
can be written as
\widetilde{X}=\widetilde{Y}\widetilde{Y}^{\top}

using various decompositions (
e.g.
, Cholesky, eigenvalue or square root, see Corollary
1.7.1
),
but
\widetilde{Y}
is not necessarily non-negative.
For any
n\geq 2
, we have
C^{n*}\subsetneq S_{n}^{+}
.

We can define the dual of a cone
C
(with regards to the scalar product) using
the formula

C^{*}=\left\{Y:~X\sprod Y\geq 0~\forall X\in C\right\}
. We already proved in
Prop.
1.3.3
that the SDP cone is self-dual,
i.e.
,
(S_{n}^{+})^{*}=S_{n}^{+}
.
Since
C^{n*}
is smaller than
S_{n}^{*}
, its dual cone might be larger than
S_{n}^{+}
. Indeed,
we introduce the
cone of copositive matrices

C^{n}=\left\{X\in S_{n}:~X\sprod\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}\geq 0~\forall\mathbf{y}\geq{\mathbf{0}}\right\}

(5.1.3)

such that
C^{n}=(C^{n*})^{*}
.

Let
\pazocal{N}^{n}\subsetneq S_{n}
be the set of non-negative symmetric matrices.
The following hierarchy of inclusions

C^{n*}\subset S_{n}^{+}\cap\pazocal{N}^{n}\subset S_{n}^{+}\subset S_{n}^{+}+\pazocal{N}^{n}\subset C^{n}

(5.1.4)

follows from two short arguments.
First, any
X\in C^{n^{*}}
satisfies
X\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
(see above)
and
X\geq{\mathbf{0}}
(see the definition
(
5.1.1
)
).
Secondly, any
S\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
and
N\geq{\mathbf{0}}
verify

S\sprod\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}\geq 0
and

N\sprod\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}\geq 0
for any
\mathbf{y}\geq{\mathbf{0}}
.

5.2
Reformulating a homogeneous quadratic program as a copositive problem

We consider the following problem with a
homogeneous objective

function and
non-negative variables
:

\displaystyle[left={(QP_{+})\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle Q\sprod\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}

(5.2.1a)

\displaystyle s.t~~

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=b

(5.2.1b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},

(5.2.1c)

where
\mathbf{a}>0
is a
strictly positive parameter
.
One should keep in mind that
\mathbf{x}
is non-negative.

5.2.1
Solving
(QP_{+})
is NP-hard

Not surprisingly, solving this program is NP hard. This
follows from the fact that it contains the maximum stable problem as a particular
case,
21
21

21

If we allow the objective
to be non-homogeneous,
we obtain a particular case of
unconstrained quadratic programming
in non-negative variables
.
This problem is NP-hard because it is at least as hard as the bi-partition
problem,
i.e.
,
one can solve the (bi-)partition problem for elements

a_{1},a_{2},\dots a_{n}
by solving

\min_{x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}\geq 0}\sum_{i}(x_{i}+x^{\prime}_{i}-1)^{2}+x_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}+\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}a_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}a_{i}\right)^{2}
. More generally, unconstrained quadratic programming is not
NP-hard because it reduces to SDP programming
(Section
3.4.3
).

as a consequence of the following result.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Consider a graph
G
with adjacency matrix
A^{G}
.
The maximum stable
\alpha(G)
can be determined by solving the following program,
which is a particular case of
(QP_{+})
from above
(
5.2.1a
)
-
(
5.2.1c
)
.

\frac{1}{\alpha(G)}=\min\left\{\left(A^{G}+I_{n}\right)\sprod\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}:~\mathbf{e}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{x}=1\right\},

(5.2.2)

where
\mathbf{e}_{n}^{\top}=\underbrace{[1~1~1\dots 1]}_{\text{n positions}}
.

Proof.

We use the technique described next. Consider any feasible solution

\mathbf{x}
of above program
(
5.2.2
)
.
We say that an edge
\{i,j\}

is
supported
by
\mathbf{x}
if
x_{i},x_{j}>0
. We will show that
if
\mathbf{x}
has more than zero supported edges, then it be transformed into a no-worse solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

that has strictly fewer supported edges.
For this, let us start by
evaluating the contribution of
x_{i}
and

x_{j}
to the objective value of edge
\{i,j\}
:

\displaystyle\texttt{val}(x_{i},x_{j})

\displaystyle=x_{i}^{2}+2x_{i}x_{j}+x_{j}^{2}+2\sum\limits_{k\in[1..n]-\{i,j\}}A^{G}_{ki}x_{k}x_{i}+2\sum\limits_{k\in[1..n]-\{i,j\}}A^{G}_{kj}x_{k}x_{j}

(5.2.3)

\displaystyle=(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}+z_{i}x_{i}+z_{j}x_{j},

(5.2.4)

where the values
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are determined by the above sums and do not depend
on
x_{i}
or
x_{j}
. Consider now the function
f:[0,x_{i}+x_{j}]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f(t)=\texttt{val}(t,x_{i}+x_{j}-t)
,
i.e.
, we replace
x_{i}
with
t
and

x_{j}
with
x_{i}+x_{j}-t
in above
(
5.2.3
)
-
(
5.2.4
)
.
This way
f(t)
can be written

f(t)=(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}+z_{i}t+z_{j}(x_{i}+x_{j}-t)=(z_{i}-z_{j})t+(x_{i}+x_{j})^{2}+z_{j}(x_{i}+x_{j})

The only-non constant term is
(z_{i}-z_{j})t
. We obtain that
f
is a
linear function that reaches its minimum value at one of
the two bounds of
[0,x_{i}+x_{j}]
,
i.e.
, a
value
\overline{t}
that minimizes
f
is either

\overline{t}=0
or
\overline{t}=x_{i}+x_{j}
.
We can say

f\left(\overline{t}\right)=\texttt{val}\left(\overline{t},x_{i}+x_{j}-\overline{t}\right)
is at least as good
as
f(x_{i})=\texttt{val}(x_{i},x_{j})
.

If we define
x^{\prime}_{i}=\overline{t},~x^{\prime}_{j}=x_{i}+x_{j}-\overline{t}

and
x^{\prime}_{k}=x_{k}~\forall k\in[1..n]-\{i,j\}
, we obtain that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is at least
as good as
\mathbf{x}
. Notice that the edge
\{i,j\}
is no longer supported in
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

because
x^{\prime}_{i}
or
x^{\prime}_{j}
is equal to zero. Any other supported edge in
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is
also supported in
\mathbf{x}
, because
x^{\prime}_{\ell}>0\implies x_{\ell}>0~\forall\ell\in[1..n]
.
This means that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
has at least one supported edge less than
\mathbf{x}
. By repeating
above operation iteratively for all supported edges, we will eventually find
some
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
that is at least as good as
\mathbf{x}
and has no supported edge.

This means that the non-zero elements of
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
generate a stable
S
of
G
. The objective
value of
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
can be written
I_{|S|}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}~\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}
, where

\overline{\mathbf{x}}
is a vector containing only the
|S|
non-zero values of
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
. We
can still say
\mathbf{e}_{|S|}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}=1
,
i.e.
, the sum of the elements
of
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
(or
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
) is one.
We now show that all elements of
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
are equal if
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
is optimal.
Assume the contrary: there is
i,j\in[1..|S|]
such that
\overline{x}_{i}\neq\overline{x}_{j}
. The
contribution of
\overline{x}_{i}
and
\overline{x}_{j}
to the objective function is

\overline{\texttt{val}}(\overline{x}_{i},\overline{x}_{j})={\overline{x}_{i}^{2}}+{\overline{x}_{j}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(2\overline{x}_{i}^{2}+2\overline{x}_{j}^{2}\right)>\frac{1}{2}\left(\overline{x}_{i}^{2}+\overline{x}_{j}^{2}+2\overline{x}_{i}\overline{x}_{j}\right)=2\left(\frac{\overline{x}_{i}+\overline{x}_{j}}{2}\right)^{2}
.
By replacing
\overline{x}_{i}
and
\overline{x}_{j}
with

\left(\frac{\overline{x}_{i}+\overline{x}_{j}}{2}\right)
we obtain a better objective
value, while still respecting the constraint (the sum of the elements
remains the same). This means that
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
is not optimal, which is a contradiction.
As such, the optimal
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
has the same value
\frac{1}{|S|}

at all positions
i\in S
and its objective value is
\sum_{i=1}^{|S|}\frac{1}{|S|^{2}}=\frac{|S|}{|S|^{2}}=\frac{1}{|S|}
.

We started from an arbitrary solution
\mathbf{x}
and we constructed
a solution
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
that is no worse than
\mathbf{x}
and that has no supported edge;
this means there is always an optimal solution with no supported edges.
We then showed that if
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
is optimal, it has to contain the value
\frac{1}{|S|}

on all positions of some stable
S
of
G
; the objective value of this
solution
is
\frac{1}{|S|}
.
But this value can only be optimal if

S
is a maximum stable of
G
with
|S|=\alpha(G)
.
We obtained that the optimum of the program
from
(
5.2.2
)
is indeed
\frac{1}{\alpha(G)}
.
It is achieved by taking
x_{i}=\frac{1}{|{S}|}
for all

i\in{S}
and
x_{i}=0
if
i\notin S

for a maximum stable
S
.
∎

According to the article ‘‘Copositive Optimization’’ by
Immanuel Bomze, Mirjam Dür and Chung-Piaw Teo,
22
22

22

Published in the
Optima
89 newsletter in august 2012,
pp. 2-8, available on line at

http://www.mathopt.org/Optima-Issues/optima89.pdf
.

the above result dates back to the 1960s. However, the
proof is personal.

5.2.2
The reformulation of
(QP_{+})
as a copositive program

Let us consider the following program associated to
(QP_{+})
from

(
5.2.1a
)
-
(
5.2.1c
)
.

\displaystyle[left={C(QP_{+})\empheqlbrace}]\min~~

\displaystyle Q\sprod X

(5.2.5a)

\displaystyle s.t~~

\displaystyle A\sprod X=(\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{\top})\sprod X=b^{2}

(5.2.5b)

\displaystyle X\in C^{n*},

(5.2.5c)

where recall
A=\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{\top}
respects

A\geq{\mathbf{0}}
and

A_{ii}>0~\forall i\in[1..n]

because

(
5.2.1a
)
-
(
5.2.1c
)
imposes
a_{i}>0\forall i\in[1..n]
,
i.e.
,

\mathbf{a}
is a strictly positive parameter.
Since the above program minimizes a
linear function, its optimum might only be achieved by an extreme point (or along an extreme
ray) of the feasible area defined by
(
5.2.5b
)
-
(
5.2.5c
)
.

Proposition 5.2.2
.

We consider a symmetric matrix
A\geq{\mathbf{0}}

with a strictly positive diagonal (
A_{ii}>0~\forall i\in[1..n]
) and some
b\in\mathbb{R}
.
The extreme solutions (vertices)
of the set below are the rank-1 matrices of the form
X=\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{\top}
with
\mathbf{u}\geq{\mathbf{0}}
. This set has no extreme rays.

[C^{n*}_{A}]=\left\{X\in C^{n*}:~A\sprod X=b^{2}\right\}

(5.2.6)

Proof.

We will prove three facts:

(i)

A rank-1 completely positive matrix
X
such that
AX=b^{2}
is an
extreme solution of
[C^{n*}_{A}]
.

(ii)

A completely positive matrix
X
of rank higher than one can not be
an extreme solution of
[C^{n*}_{A}]
.

(iii)

The set
[C^{n*}_{A}]
has
no
extreme ray.

(i)

We first prove that a rank-1 matrix
X=\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}
(with
\mathbf{y}\geq{\mathbf{0}}
)
such that
AX=b^{2}
is an
extreme solution of this
[C^{n*}_{A}]
set. Assume the contrary: there is symmetric non-zero
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

such that
X-M,X+M\in[C^{n*}_{A}]
. Based on
(
5.1.1
)
, we
can write
X-M=Y_{a}Y_{a}^{\top}
and
X+M=Y_{b}Y_{b}^{\top}
; combining the two, we obtain
X=\frac{1}{2}[Y_{a}Y_{b}][Y_{a}Y_{b}]^{\top}
. But since
rank(X)=rank\left(\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}\right)=1
, we can apply
Prop.
A.1.8
to conclude

rank(X)=rank\left([Y_{a}Y_{b}][Y_{a}Y_{b}]^{\top}\right)=rank([Y_{a}Y_{b}])
, and so,
we have
rank([Y_{a}Y_{b}])=1
. The columns of
Y_{a}
and
Y_{b}
need to be multiples
of
\mathbf{y}
. The matrices
X
and
X-M
are multiples of
\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^{\top}
