be determined at point (a) below. However, this
\mathbf{w}_{i}
vector
leads to the following values on the first row of
M^{i-1}
.

(a)

M^{i-1}_{1,1}=\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}+2\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}\Big(-\left(Q+P_{i}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{u}_{i}+z\mathbf{u}_{i}\Big)=\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}-2\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}-2\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}P_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{i}+2\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}z\mathbf{u}_{i}=-\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}+2z\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{u}_{i}=-\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}+2z
, where we used
(
C.2.11
)
to cancel the
P_{i}^{\top}
term.
We set
z=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q\mathbf{u}_{i}\right)
to make
M^{i-1}_{1,1}=0
,
but larger values can also be chosen.

(b)

M^{i-1}_{1,[2..n-i+1]}=\left(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}Q+\left(-\left(Q+P_{i}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{u}_{i}+z\mathbf{u}_{i}\right)^{\top}+\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}P_{i}\right)\left[\mathbf{u}_{i+1},\dots\mathbf{u}_{p},B\right]=z\mathbf{u}_{i}^{\top}\left[\mathbf{u}_{i+1},\dots\mathbf{u}_{p},B\right]={{\mathbf{0}}_{1\times(n-i)}}.

The resulting first row of
M^{i-1}
is filled with zeros, and so needs to be the first column by symmetry.
Recalling
(
C.2.1
)
, we have
M^{i-1}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&{\mathbf{0}}\\
{\mathbf{0}}&M^{i}\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.
Using the induction hypothesis

M^{i}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
, we obtain
M^{i-1}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
, which finishes the induction step. At the last iteration, we obtain
M^{0}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
, which
is enough to guarantee
Q_{0}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
using arguments discussed above. Recalling
(
C.2.5
)
and
(
C.2.2
)
, this finishes the proof.
∎

C.2.2
Refining the

Branch-and-bound

for
equality-constrained binary quadratic
programming from Section
6.3.4.2

An approach like in Section
6.3.4.2

can be found in the article
‘‘Improving the performance of standard solvers for quadratic 0-1 programs by a tight convex reformulation: The QCR method’’
by Alain Billionnet, Sourour Elloumi and Marie-Christine Plateau.
38
38

38

Published in
Discrete Applied Mathematics
in 2009, vol 157 (6), pp. 1185-1197,
a draft is available at
http://cedric.cnam.fr/fichiers/RC1120.pdf
.

They use the redundant constraints from Example
6.3.5
which yield the
optimum value

OPT(SDP(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}}))=\left(PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})\right)
as stated in
Remark
6.3.9
.
However, instead of using
{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
, they work with a restricted
version

{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})

of this program in which they also impose
x_{i}\in[0,1]
, equivalent to
x^{2}_{i}\leq x_{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]
.
However, they show that this program has the same objective value
OPT(SDP(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}}))

using an argument based on the Slater’s interiority condition, stating that the
total dual Lagrangian of

{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
has no duality gap—see the
implication
(D1)\to(D2)
. However, we can here use a different argument.

If we construct

{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
,

{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$X$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
and

SDP^{[0-1]}(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})

from resp.
{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
,

{\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$X$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
and

SDP(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})

by adding constraints
x_{i}^{2}\leq x_{i}
(or resp.
X_{ii}\leq x_{i}
)
\forall i\in[1..n]
in
resp.
(
6.3.4.1
)
,
(
6.2.5
)
and
(
6.2.2a
)
-
(
6.2.2e
)
, then
the following hierarchy holds and it naturally collapses:

\displaystyle OPT\left(SDP(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})\right)

\displaystyle=OPT\left({\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\right)

(C.2.12a)

\displaystyle\leq OPT\left({\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\mathbf{x}$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\right)

(C.2.12b)

\displaystyle=OPT\left({\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$X$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\right)

(C.2.12c)

\displaystyle\leq OPT\left(SDP^{[0-1]}(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})\right)

(C.2.12d)

\displaystyle=OPT\left(SDP(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})\right)

(C.2.12e)

Proof.

The first equality
(
C.2.12a
)
is taken from
(
6.3.10
)
. The inequality
(
C.2.12b
)
follows
from the fact that the “
[0-1]
” version of the partial Lagrangian (a
minimization program) is more constrained.
The equality
(
C.2.12c
)
is due to the fact that the quadratic factor

Q_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}
of both programs (in variables
X
or
\mathbf{x}
) is SDP and that
X=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\top}

respects all constraints of the SDP program in variables
X
. The inequality
(
C.2.12d
)

follows from the fact that
\left({\scalebox{1.4}{$\mathscr{L}$}}^{[0-1]}_{PL^{\scalebox{1.2}{$X$}}(QP_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}})}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*},\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\right)
is a Lagrangian of

(SDP^{[0-1]}(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}}))
. Finally,
(
C.2.12e
)
holds because
(SDP(P_{\scalebox{0.55}{=}}))
already
integrates binary constraints
X_{ii}=x_{i}~\forall i\in[1..n]
.
∎

More advanced convexifications can be found in the work of A. Billionnet,
S. Elloumi and
A. Lambert,
e.g.
, see papers
‘‘Extending the QCR method to general mixed integer programs.’’
and
‘‘Exact quadratic convex reformulations of mixed-integer quadratically constrained problems’’.
39
39

39

Both published in
Mathematical Programming
, resp .in
2012 (vol. 131(1), pp. 381-401) and 2016 (vol 158(1), pp 235-266).

However, for the moment, such methods lie outside the scope of this non-research
document;
further progress is unessential for now.

C.3
A convex function with an asymmetric Hessian

We next provide an example of a convex function with an asymmetric Hessian. This
shows that a statement like “A twice differentiable function is convex if
and only if its Hessian is SDP” is technically not complete, because a convex
function can have an asymmetric non-SDP Hessian.
This case is omitted from certain textbooks
(see a reference in the first paragraph of Section
1.8
)
but we addressed it in our work by requiring the Hessian to
be symmetric in Prop.
1.8.1
.

Example C.3.1
.

The following function
f
is convex for any
\mu\geq 9
and has a
non-symmetric Hessian in
{\mathbf{0}}
, i.e.,
\nabla^{2}f({\mathbf{0}})
is not symmetric.

f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{x^{3}y}{x^{2}+y^{2}}+\mu x^{2}+\mu y^{2}&\text{if }(x,y)\neq(0,0)\\
0&\text{if }(x,y)=0\\
\end{cases}

(C.3.1)

Proof.

The gradient of
f
at

(0,0)
can not be computed algebraically. We can however obtain

\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}(0,0)=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{f(\varepsilon,0)-f(0)}{\varepsilon}=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\mu\varepsilon^{2}}{\varepsilon}=0
and similarly

\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}(0,0)=0
.
The gradient of
f
is thus:

\nabla f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{x^{2}y\left(x^{2}+3y^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}+2\mu x,\dfrac{x^{3}\left(x^{2}-y^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{2}}+2\mu y\right)&\text{if }(x,y)\neq(0,0)\\
(0,0)&\text{if }(x,y)=0\\
\end{cases}

Let us now calculate the Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})
. As above, we apply the
derivative formula to calculate
\nabla^{2}f(0,0)
:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x\partial x}\Big(0,0\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\Big(\varepsilon,0\Big)-\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\Big(0,0\Big)}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{2\mu\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}=2\mu

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial y\partial x}\Big(0,0\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\Big(0,\varepsilon\Big)-\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}\Big(0,0\Big)}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{0}{\varepsilon}=0

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x\partial y}\Big(0,0\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}\Big(\varepsilon,0\Big)-\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}\Big(0,0\Big)}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{~~\dfrac{\varepsilon^{3}(\varepsilon^{2}-0^{2})}{(\varepsilon^{2}+0^{2})^{2}}~~}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}=1

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial y\partial y}\Big(0,0\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}\Big(0,\varepsilon\Big)-\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}\Big(0,0\Big)}{\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{2\mu\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}=2\mu

The Hessian of
f
is thus:

\nabla^{2}f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{2xy^{3}\left(3y^{2}-x^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{3}}+2\mu&\dfrac{x^{2}\left(x^{4}+6y^{2}x^{2}-3y^{4}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{3}}\\
\dfrac{x^{2}\left(x^{4}+6y^{2}x^{2}-3y^{4}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{3}}&\dfrac{2x^{3}y\left(y^{2}-3x^{2}\right)}{\left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)^{3}}+2\mu\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }(x,y)\neq(0,0)\\
\begin{bmatrix}2\mu&1\\
0&2\mu\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }(x,y)=0\\
\end{cases}

All fractions in the Hessian have the form
\dfrac{p_{1}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}
, where

p_{1}
and
p
are both
homogeneous polynomials of degree
6. We easily obtain
\dfrac{p_{1}(tx,ty)}{p(tx,ty)}=\dfrac{t^{6}p_{1}(x,y)}{t^{6}p(x,y)}=\dfrac{p_{1}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}
. The graph of such fraction can be seen as a surface
that has the same height (value) on each ray starting from (but not touching)
the origin. The image of
\dfrac{p_{1}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}
over
\mathbb{R}^{2}-\{0\}
is equal to the
image of
\dfrac{p_{1}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}
over unit circle
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
, which needs to
be bounded.

We now calculate the bounds of the fractions. First, notice

\dfrac{p_{1}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}=p_{1}(x,y)
over the unit circle, since

p(x,y)=(x^{2}+y^{2})^{6}
. Each monomial of degree 6 of
p_{1}(x,y)
belongs
to the interval
[-1,1]
. Using this, we obtain, for instance, that
\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x\partial x}-2\mu\in\big[\min\left(2xy^{3}\left(3y^{2}-x^{2}\right)\right),\max\left(2xy^{3}\left(3y^{2}-x^{2}\right)\right)\big]\subset[-8,8]
when
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
. By applying the same approach on all fractions, we
obtain:

-\begin{bmatrix}8&10\\
10&8\end{bmatrix}\leq\nabla^{2}f(x,y)-\begin{bmatrix}2\mu&0\\
0&2\mu\end{bmatrix}\leq\begin{bmatrix}8&10\\
10&8\end{bmatrix}

By taking any
\mu\geq 9
, we obtain

\nabla^{2}f(x,y)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
for
(x,y)\neq(0,0)
. On the other hand, we
can
never
state
\nabla^{2}(0,0)\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
because
\nabla^{2}(0,0)

is not symmetric. We thus need to use

\nabla^{2}(0,0)+\nabla^{2}(0,0)^{\top}\succeq{\mathbf{0}}
.

The idea is taken from the last pages of the article
‘‘On second derivatives of convex functions’’
by Richard Dudley,
40
40

40

Published in

Mathematica Scandinavica
in 1978, vol 41, pp 159–174,
available on-line as of 2017 at

http://www.mscand.dk/article/download/11710/9726
,
see also the discussion on the on-line math forum

https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/1181713/convex-function-with-non-symmetric-hessian
.

but we are the first to calculate an explicit minimum value of
\mu
.
The derivatives were calculated at
http://www.derivative-calculator.net
.
∎

C.4
The separating hyperplane theorem

C.4.1
General theorems and their reduction to a particular case

Theorem C.4.1
.

(Hyperplane separation theorem) Given two
disjoint convex sets

X,Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exist a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a real number
c
such
that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq c\geq\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},

(C.4.1)

for any
\mathbf{x}\in X
and
\mathbf{y}\in Y
. The hyperplane
\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:~\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{u}=c\}

separates
X
and
Y
.

Proof.

We will show that the general theorem reduces to a simpler theorem version
in which

Y=\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
,
i.e.
, the hyperplane
{\mathbf{0}}
-separation Theorem

C.4.2
.

Consider the set
Z=X-Y=\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}:~\mathbf{x}\in X,~\mathbf{y}\in Y\}
. The set
Z
is convex:
take
\mathbf{z}_{a}=\mathbf{x}_{a}-\mathbf{y}_{a}
,
\mathbf{z}_{b}=\mathbf{x}_{b}-\mathbf{y}_{b}
and any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and observe
\alpha\mathbf{z}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{z}_{b}=\alpha(\mathbf{x}_{a}-\mathbf{y}_{a})+(1-\alpha)(\mathbf{x}_{b}-\mathbf{y}_{b})=\alpha\mathbf{x}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{x}_{b}-(\alpha\mathbf{y}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{b})
. Since
X
and
Y
are convex,

\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\alpha\mathbf{x}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{x}_{b}\in X
and
\mathbf{y}_{\alpha}=\alpha\mathbf{y}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{b}\in Y
, and
so,
\mathbf{z}_{a}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{z}_{b}=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{y}_{\alpha}\in Z
,
i.e.
,
Z
is convex.

We apply the hyperplane
{\mathbf{0}}
-separation Theorem
C.4.2
on
Z

and
{\mathbf{0}}
(observe
{\mathbf{0}}\notin Z
because
X
and
Y
are disjoint) and
obtain there is a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{z}\geq 0~\forall\mathbf{z}\in Z
. This means that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X\text{ and }\forall\mathbf{y}\in Y.

(C.4.2)

We obtain
\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in X}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in Y}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y}
,
because otherwise

(
C.4.2
)
would be violated by some

\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
such that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}
is close enough to

\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in X}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y}
is close enough to

\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in Y}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y}
. Taking
c=\frac{\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in X}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}+\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in Y}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y}}{2}
,
(
C.4.2
)
can be written in the form
(
C.4.1
)
.
∎

Theorem C.4.2
.

(Hyperplane
{\mathbf{0}}
-separation theorem) Given convex set

X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
that does not contain
{\mathbf{0}}
, there exist a non-zero

\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq 0,

for any
\mathbf{x}\in X
.

We give two proofs. The first one is based on induction and it takes
a bit more than one page. It is a personal proof; I
doubt it can also be found in classical textbooks. The second
proof takes 2.5 pages and it follows well-established arguments (some of them using convergent sequences) that I could found on the
Internet.
41
41

41

I used the Wikipedia
article

en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperplane_separation_theorem
and the course
of Peter Norman
www.unc.edu/~normanp/890part4.pdf
.

The first proof essentially relies on
Theorem
C.4.4
from Appendix
C.4.2
;
the second one
relies on
Theorem
C.4.5
and Theorem
C.4.6

from Appendix
C.4.3
.

Proof 1

For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
let
f(\mathbf{u})
be the largest
t
such that
t\mathbf{u}\in X
, or
-\infty
if no
t\mathbf{u}

belongs to
X
. If
f(\mathbf{u})>0
for all non-zero
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
{\mathbf{0}}\in X
: it is enough
to take
\mathbf{u}
and
-\mathbf{u}
and observe that the segment joining
f(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}
and

f(-\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{u}
contains
{\mathbf{0}}
. Since
X
does not contain
{\mathbf{0}}
, there exist
some non-zero
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
f(\mathbf{u})\leq 0
. The theorem then follows
from applying Theorem
C.4.4
.
∎

Proof 2

We first prove that the closure
\overline{X}
of
X
(
i.e.
, the set
X
along with
all its limit points) is convex. Take any
\mathbf{x},~\mathbf{y}\in\overline{X}
and consider
two sequences
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
and
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}
in
X
that converge to
\mathbf{x}
and resp.
\mathbf{y}
.
Such sequences always exist because, by definition,
\overline{X}

is the set of the limit points of all sequences
of
X
.
Take any
\alpha\in[0,1]
;
it is enough to
show
\alpha\mathbf{x}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}\in\overline{X}
to prove that
\overline{X}
is convex.
We have
\mathbf{z}_{i}=\alpha\mathbf{x}_{i}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{i}\in X
, because
X
is convex. We next observe that

z=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}z_{i}\in\overline{X}
, because
\overline{X}
contains all
limit points of
X
. But
\alpha\mathbf{x}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}=\alpha\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{i}+(1-\alpha)\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{y}_{i}=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{z}_{i}=\mathbf{z}\in\overline{X}
, and so,

\overline{X}
is convex.

If
{\mathbf{0}}
does not belong to the closure
\overline{X}
, then the conclusion follows from the Simple Separation
Theorem
C.4.5
applied on
{\mathbf{0}}
and
\overline{X}
.
If
{\mathbf{0}}
belongs to the closure of
X
, the conclusion follows from the
Simple Supporting Hyperplane Theorem
C.4.6
applied on

\overline{X}
and
{\mathbf{0}}
as a boundary point of
\overline{X}
(
{\mathbf{0}}
does
not belong to the interior, because it does not belong to
X
).
∎

The following variant can be generally useful, but we do not need it in this
document.

Theorem C.4.3
.

(Separation theorem for open set
X
) Given two
disjoint convex sets

X,Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
X
is open, there exist a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a real number
c
such
that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}>c\geq\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},\forall\mathbf{x}\in X,~\mathbf{y}\in Y

(C.4.3)

The closure
\overline{X}
of
X
satisfies

\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}\geq c\geq\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},\forall\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in\overline{X},~\mathbf{y}\in Y

(C.4.4)

Proof.

Using the standard hyperplane separation Theorem

C.4.1
, there is a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and some
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq c\geq\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},\forall\mathbf{x}\in X,~\mathbf{y}\in Y

(C.4.5)

For the sake of contradiction, assume there exists some
\mathbf{x}\in X
such that
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}=c
. Since
X
is
open,
X
contains an open ball around
\mathbf{x}
, and so, for a sufficiently small

\epsilon>0
, we have
\mathbf{x}-\epsilon\mathbf{v}\in X
. But
\mathbf{v}\sprod(\mathbf{x}-\epsilon\mathbf{v})=c-\epsilon|\mathbf{v}|^{2}<c
which contradicts
(
C.4.5
)
. The assumption

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}=c
was false, and so,
(
C.4.5
)
becomes
(
C.4.3
)
.

We still have to prove
(
C.4.4
)
. Assume there is some

\overline{\mathbf{x}}
in
\overline{X}
such that
\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}<c
. Since

\overline{\mathbf{x}}
has to be the limit point of some sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
with
elements
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in X~\forall i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we deduce that

\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}
. For any

\epsilon>0
there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
|\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}|<\epsilon~\forall i\geq m
. Taking any

\epsilon<c-\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}
, we have

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{m}<c
, which contradicts
(
C.4.3
)
. The
assumption
\mathbf{v}\sprod\overline{\mathbf{x}}<c
was false, which proves

(
C.4.4
)
.
∎

C.4.2
Proving the theorem using personal arguments

Theorem C.4.4
.

(Hyperplane
{\mathbf{0}}
-separation theorem in presence of open
rays) Consider convex set
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(that may contain
{\mathbf{0}}
or not)
and let
f(\mathbf{u})
be the largest
t
such
that
t\mathbf{u}\in X
for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
f(\mathbf{u})\leq 0
, we say that
ray
\mathbf{u}
is open, because there is no
\epsilon>0
such that
\epsilon\mathbf{u}\in X
. If there is at least an open ray, then there exist some
non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq 0,~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X.

Proof.

We proceed by induction. We first prove it for
n=2
using the notion of angle.

Lemma C.4.4.1
.

The theorem holds for
n=2
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we consider the open ray
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is unitary.
We associate
\mathbf{u}
with an angle
\theta=0
.
Using a slight notation abuse, let
f(\theta)=f(\mathbf{u}_{\theta})
, where
\mathbf{u}_{\theta}

is an unitary vector of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that makes an angle of
\theta
with
\mathbf{u}

measured clockwise. Technically,
\mathbf{u}_{\theta}

satisfies
\mathbf{u}\sprod\mathbf{u}_{\theta}=\cos(\theta)
and it
is the first unitary vector with this property found by moving
clockwise from
\mathbf{u}
.
Let
\Theta
be the set of angles

\theta
for which
f(\theta)>0
.

\Theta
belongs to segment
(0,2\pi)
because it does not contain
\theta=0
and it might be open. However,
\Theta
needs to have
an infimum
\inf(\Theta)
and a supremum
\sup(\Theta)
,
see also Prop.
C.4.7
.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that

\sup(\Theta)-\inf(\Theta)>\pi
. This means there are two angles

\theta_{M},\theta_{m}\in\Theta
close enough
to
\sup(\Theta)
and resp.
\inf(\Theta)
so that
\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}>\pi
.
By convexity, the segment that joins

f(\theta_{M})\mathbf{u}_{\theta_{M}}
and

f(\theta_{m})\mathbf{u}_{\theta_{m}}
is included in
X
and it also
intersects the segment
[{\mathbf{0}},\mathbf{u}]
in some point
\epsilon\mathbf{u}

with
\epsilon>0
. This contradicts the fact that
\mathbf{u}
is an open ray.

We can now consider
\sup(\Theta)-\inf(\Theta)\leq\pi
. The line

\left\{t\mathbf{u}^{*}:~t\in\mathbb{R},\mathbf{u}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}-\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\right\}
that goes
through
{\mathbf{0}}
and makes an angle of
\inf(\Theta)
with
\mathbf{u}
(clockwise) has the whole

X
on one side; all points in
X
make an angle in

[\inf(\Theta),\sup(\Theta)]
with
\mathbf{u}
(clockwise). We can take
\mathbf{v}
one of the two vectors perpendicular to
\mathbf{u}^{*}
in
{\mathbf{0}}

and obtain
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in X
.
∎

Now consider
n>2
. Take a 2-dimensional sub-space
S_{2}
that contains the unitary
vector
\mathbf{u}
,
i.e.
,
S_{2}=\left\{t\mathbf{u}+t^{\prime}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:~t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
for some unitary
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
such that

\mathbf{u}\sprod\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=0
. The intersection of two convex sets is convex, and so,

X_{2}=S_{2}\cap X
is convex. Using above lemma for
n=2
, there exists some
unitary
\mathbf{v}_{2}\in S_{2}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2}\geq 0,~\forall\mathbf{x}_{2}\in X_{2}
.
Notice
that
f(-\mathbf{v}_{2})\leq 0
because all
-t\mathbf{v}_{2}
with
t>0
do not belong to
X
or

X_{2}
, since
\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod(-t\mathbf{v}_{2})<0
.

Take any
unitary

\mathbf{u}_{2}\in S_{2}
such that

\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{u}_{2}=0.

(C.4.6)

Consider the
(n-1)
-dimensional sub-space
S_{n-1}\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{n}
perpendicular
on
\mathbf{u}_{2}
,
i.e.
,

S_{n-1}=\{\mathbf{x}\in X:~\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}=0\}
.
Observe
\mathbf{v}_{2}
and
-\mathbf{v}_{2}
belong to
S_{n-1}
, using
(
C.4.6
)
.
We now project the whole space
X
on
S_{n-1}
,
i.e.
, we obtain the set

X_{n-1}=\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}):~\mathbf{x}\in X\}
. One can easily check
that all elements of
X_{n-1}
satisfy
\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}))=\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}=0
.

We now define function
f_{n-1}:S_{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
in the same style
as
f
,
i.e.
,
f_{n-1}(\mathbf{s}_{n-1})

is the smallest
t
for which
t\mathbf{s}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
, for any
\mathbf{s}_{n-1}\in S_{n-1}
. We showed above that
f(-\mathbf{v}_{2})\leq 0
. We can
also prove
f_{n-1}(-\mathbf{v}_{2})\leq 0
. Recall we have

\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2}\geq 0,~\forall\mathbf{x}_{2}\in X_{2}
. Consider now the projection

\mathbf{x}_{n-1}=\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2})
and notice that
\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{n-1}=\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod(\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2}))=\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{u}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2})=\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{2}\geq 0
(we used

(
C.4.6
)
for the last equality). The elements
-t\mathbf{v}_{2}

with
t>0
can not belong to the projection of
X_{2}
, because

\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod(-t\mathbf{v}_{2})<0
and the elements
\mathbf{x}_{n-1}
of the projection
verify
\mathbf{v}_{2}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\geq 0
. Finally, remark we do not lose generality by
restricting the argument to the projections of
X_{2}
: all elements of
X
that
could project on
-t\mathbf{v}_{2}
could only belong to
X_{2}
,
i.e.
, the space generated
by
\mathbf{v}_{2}
and
\mathbf{u}_{2}
.

We can easily check that
X_{n-1}
is convex. Consider
\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
as the projection of

\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+a\mathbf{u}_{2}\in X
and
\mathbf{y}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
as the projection of

\mathbf{y}_{n-1}+b\mathbf{u}_{2}\in X
. Using the
convexity of
X
, the following holds for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
:
\alpha(\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+a\mathbf{u}_{2})+(1-\alpha)(\mathbf{y}_{n-1}+b\mathbf{u}_{2})\in X
. We can re-write this as:

\alpha\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{n-1}+(\alpha a+(1-\alpha)b)\mathbf{u}_{2}\in X
, and so,

\alpha\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
(one can easily verify that the
scalar product of this with
\mathbf{u}_{2}
is zero).

We now apply the induction hypothesis on set
X_{n-1}
with
f_{n-1}(-\mathbf{v}_{2})\leq 0
in the sub-space
S_{n-1}
(notice this is a full
(n-1)
-dimensional space
where all elements can be written as a linear combination of a canonical basis
perpendicular to
\mathbf{u}_{2}
). We obtain there is non-zero
\mathbf{v}_{n-1}\in S_{n-1}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{n-1}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\geq 0
for all
\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
. This means that
\mathbf{v}_{n-1}\sprod(\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+a\mathbf{u}_{2})\geq 0
for any
\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1}
and any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, because

\mathbf{v}_{n-1}
is perpendicular on
\mathbf{u}_{2}
. It is
easy to check that
X\subseteq\{\mathbf{x}_{n-1}+a\mathbf{u}_{2}:~\mathbf{x}_{n-1}\in X_{n-1},~a\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
This is enough to conclude that
\mathbf{v}_{n-1}\mathbf{x}\geq 0
for any
\mathbf{x}\in X
.
∎

C.4.3
Proving the theorem using well-established textbook arguments

Theorem C.4.5
.

(Simple separation theorem)
Given convex closed set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{y}\notin X
, there exist a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}>\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{y},~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X.

(C.4.7)

Proof.

We need the following lemma.

Lemma C.4.5.1
.

Given closed convex set
X
, there exist a unique
\mathbf{x}\in X
such that

|\mathbf{x}|=\inf\{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|:~\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X\}
, where
|\cdot|
is the norm (length), e.g.,

|\mathbf{x}|=\sqrt{\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{x}}
.

Proof.

The Wikipedia proof is kind of magical for my taste, with a few tricks arising
rather out of the blue. I provide a more natural and even simpler (without
Cauchy sequences) proof.

Let
\delta=\inf\{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|:~\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X\}
. We first show
X
contains
a sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
such that
\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}|\mathbf{x}_{i}|=\delta
. For
instance, we can consider
\epsilon_{1}=1
,
\epsilon_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
,

\epsilon_{3}=\frac{1}{3}
,

\dots
. For each
\epsilon_{i}
(
i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
),
X
needs to contain some
\mathbf{x}_{i}

such that
|\mathbf{x}_{i}|<\delta+\epsilon_{i}
, because otherwise we would have

\delta+\epsilon_{i}\leq\inf\{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|:~\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X\}
, impossible since

\delta=\inf\{|\mathbf{x}^{\prime}|:~\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X\}
. The sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
constructed this way
satisfies
\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}|\mathbf{x}_{i}|=\delta
.

This sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
of elements of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
needs to contain a convergent
subsequence using the Bolzano-Weierstrass Theorem
C.4.9
,

i.e.
, there exists a sub-sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}\}
such that

\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}=\mathbf{x}
. Since
X
is closed, it contains all
limit points, and so,
\mathbf{x}\in X
. It is not hard now to check that the sub-sequence

\{|\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}|\}
converges to
\delta
. Since for any
\epsilon
there exists

m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
|\mathbf{x}_{j}|<\delta+\epsilon~\forall j\geq m
, there must be
some
n_{m}\geq m
(the sub-sequence is infinite) such that

|\mathbf{x}_{n_{j}}|<\delta+\epsilon~\forall n_{j}\geq n_{m}
. This confirms
\delta=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}|\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}|=|\mathbf{x}|
.

We still need to show that
\mathbf{x}
is the unique element of minimum norm. Suppose
there exists
\mathbf{y}\in X-\{\mathbf{x}\}
such that
|\mathbf{y}|=\delta
. By convexity,
\frac{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}{2}\in X
. We can calculate
|\frac{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}{2}|^{2}=\frac{\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}\sprod\mathbf{y}+2\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}}{4}=\frac{\delta^{2}+\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}}{2}
. We will show

\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}<\delta^{2}
. For this, it is enough to observe that
0<|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{2}=\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}\sprod\mathbf{y}-2\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}=2\delta^{2}-2\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}
,
i.e.
,

\mathbf{x}\sprod\mathbf{y}<\delta^{2}
. We obtained that
|\frac{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}}{2}|^{2}<\delta^{2}
,
contradiction. There is no
\mathbf{y}\neq\mathbf{x}
in
X
such that
|\mathbf{y}|=\delta
.
∎

We will first prove the theorem for
\mathbf{y}={\mathbf{0}}
and then we will use a simple
translation argument to extend it for an arbitrary
\mathbf{y}
. Using the lemma, let us
take
\mathbf{v}\in X
of minimum norm
\delta>0
(because
\mathbf{y}={\mathbf{0}}\notin X
). We will
prove the following:

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}>0,~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X

(C.4.8)

Consider any
\mathbf{x}\in X
and write
\Delta=\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}
.
Keeping in mind the goal of showing
\mathbf{v}\sprod\Delta\geq 0
,
consider
a function
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f(t)=\left(\mathbf{v}+t\Delta\right)\sprod\left(\mathbf{v}+t\Delta\right)
.
By convexity, we simply have

\mathbf{v}+t\Delta\in X~\forall t\in[0,1]
, and,
using the above lemma,
we also obtain
f(t)>f(0)=\gamma~\forall t>0
.
This means the derivative in 0 can not be negative;
we need to have
f^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
.
Since
f^{\prime}(t)=2\mathbf{v}\sprod\Delta+t^{2}\Delta\sprod\Delta
,
this means

\mathbf{v}\sprod\Delta\geq 0
,
enough to show
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{v}\sprod\left(\mathbf{v}+\Delta\right)=\gamma+\mathbf{v}\sprod\Delta\geq\gamma>0
.

Finally, if
\mathbf{y}\neq{\mathbf{0}}
, it is enough to consider set
X^{\prime}=\{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}:~\mathbf{x}\in X\}
which
is convex and does not contain
{\mathbf{0}}
. We can apply the theorem for
{\mathbf{0}}

and
X^{\prime}
and we obtain there is
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{v}\sprod(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})>0
,

\forall\mathbf{x}\in X
, which is equivalent to
(
C.4.7
)
as needed.
∎

Theorem C.4.6
.

(Simple supporting hyperplane theorem)
Given convex closed set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
{\mathbf{0}}
is a boundary point of

X
, there exist a non-zero
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}\geq 0,~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X.

(C.4.9)

Proof.

Since
{\mathbf{0}}
is a boundary point, there exists a sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
of exterior
points (
i.e.
,
\mathbf{x}_{i}\notin X,~\forall i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
) that converges to
{\mathbf{0}}
.
Using the simple separation Theorem
C.4.5
, for each
\mathbf{x}_{i}
there
exists a non-zero unitary
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{x}>\mathbf{v}_{i}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{i}~\forall\mathbf{x}\in X.

(C.4.10)

Without loss of generality, we can consider all
\mathbf{v}_{i}
are unitary,
i.e.
,

|\mathbf{v}_{i}|=1~\forall i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. As such, the sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{i}\}
is bounded, and
so, we can apply the
Bolzano-Weierstrass Theorem
C.4.9
to conclude that

\{\mathbf{v}_{i}\}
contains a
convergent sub-sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}\}
such that
\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}=\mathbf{v}
.

Assume there is some
\mathbf{x}\in X
such that
\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}=-a<0
.
We derive a contradiction using a limiting argument.
We take an
\epsilon<|a|
;
since
\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}\sprod\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{v}\sprod\mathbf{x}=-a
,
there exits some
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}\sprod\mathbf{x}\in[-a-\epsilon,-a+\epsilon]
for all
i\geq m
.
Applying
(
C.4.10
)
, we obtain that
all these
i\geq m
satisfy
\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}<-a+\epsilon<0
.
This contradicts the fact that

\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}=0
. Indeed, if
\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}
can become
arbitrarily close to
\mathbf{v}
while at the same time
\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}
becomes
arbitrarily close to
{\mathbf{0}}
, then the product
\mathbf{v}_{n_{i}}\sprod\mathbf{x}_{n_{i}}
can
also become arbitrarily close to
0
.
∎

C.4.3.1  Convergence theorems on sequences

We need several convergence results on sequences for the classical proof of the
hyperplane separation theorem,
i.e.
, in particular for Theorems

C.4.5
and
C.4.6
.

Proposition C.4.7
.

Any bounded set
S\subsetneq\mathbb{R}
has a
unique finite least upper bound (or supremum)
\sup(S)
. Equivalently,
a unique
\inf(S)
also exists and is finite.

Proof.

To avoid unessential complication, we will assume that
S
contains at least a
positive number. Any set
S^{\prime}
can be transformed to this form by applying
a simple translation
S=\{s^{\prime}-s^{\prime}_{0}:~s\in S^{\prime}\}
for some fixed
s^{\prime}_{0}\in S^{\prime}
. If
\sup(S)

exists, then
\sup(S^{\prime})=\sup(S)+s^{\prime}_{0}
.

We will determine
\sup(S)
as a real number
a
written in the decimal
expansion as
a=a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\dots
with potentially infinite number of digits.
We can suppose that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}99\dots 9
with
a_{n}\neq 9
is equal to

a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}(a_{n}+1)
.
We choose
the decimals as follows.

–

a_{0}
is the greatest integer that is not a strict upper bound for

S
(
i.e.
, that is not strictly greater than all elements of
S
). A finite
a_{0}
value must
exists because
S
is bounded.

–

a_{1}
is the greatest digit such that
a_{0}.a_{1}
is not a strict upper bound of
S

–

a_{2}
is the greatest digit such that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}
is not a strict upper bound of
S

\vdots

–

a_{n}
is the greatest digit such that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}
is not a strict upper bound of
S

\vdots

We now prove that
a=a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\dots
is an upper bound of
S
. Assume there
exists an
s\in S
such that
s>a
. This means there exists an index
n
such that

s
can be written
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}s_{n}s_{n+1}s_{n+2}\dots
where

s_{n}>a_{n}
. Since
a_{n}
is the greatest digit such that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}
is not a strict upper bound for
S
, we obtain that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}s_{n}
is
a strict upper bound for
S
. This implies that
s\geq a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}s_{n}
is
also a strict upper bound of
S
, which contradicts
s\in S
. There can be no
s\in S

such that
s>a
.

To prove the uniqueness,
we still have to show that
a
is the minimum upper bound,
i.e.
,
there is no other upper bound
a^{\prime}<a
.
For the sake of contradiction,
assume there exists such an upper bound

a^{\prime}<a
; it can be written
a^{\prime}=a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}a^{\prime}_{n}a^{\prime}_{n+1}a^{\prime}_{n+2}\dots
such
that
a^{\prime}_{n}<a_{n}
for some
n
and
a^{\prime}_{n+1},a^{\prime}_{n+2}\dots
are not all
9
—such a
number would reduce to
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}(a^{\prime}_{n}+1)
. We thus
obtain that
a^{\prime}<a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}a_{n}
. Since
a^{\prime}
is an upper bound,

a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}a_{n}
needs to be a strict upper bound. This contradicts
the choice of
a_{n}
as the greatest digit such that
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}a_{n}

is
not
a strict upper bound of
S
.

By combining the two above paragraphs, we obtain that
a=a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\dots
is
the least upper bound
\sup(S)
. Recall we can suppose that

a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}99\dots 9
with
a_{n}\neq 9
is equal to
a_{0}.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n-1}(a_{n}+1)
.
∎

Proposition C.4.8
.

Any bounded monotone sequence
\{a_{i}\}
of real numbers is
convergent.

Proof.

Since the sequence is monotone, we can
consider it is non-decreasing, the non-increasing case being completely
analogous.
Let
A=\left\{a_{i}:~i\in\{1,2,\dots\infty\}\right\}
.
Since
A
is bounded, the least upper bound property (Prop.
C.4.7
)
states that
a=\sup(A)
exists and is finite.
For any
\epsilon>0
, there needs to exist some positive integer
n
such
that
a_{n}>a-\epsilon
, because otherwise
a-\epsilon
would be an upper bound
lower than
\sup(A)
, impossible. Since
\{a_{i}\}
is non-decreasing, all integers

n^{\prime}>n
verify
a_{n^{\prime}}\geq a_{n}>a-\epsilon
. This is exactly the definition of
the fact that
\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}a_{i}=a
.
∎

Theorem C.4.9
.

(Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem)
Any bounded sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
contains a convergent subsequence.

Proof.

We first show the theorem for
n=1
.

Lemma C.4.9.1
.

Any bounded sequence
\{x_{i}\}
of
\mathbb{R}
contains a convergent subsequence.

Proof.

We consider the set of maxima
N=\left\{i\in\left\{1,2,\dots\right\}:~x_{j}<x_{i},\forall j>i\right\}
.
We distinguish three cases depending on the cardinal of
N
:

1.

If
N=\emptyset
, then
for any index
n_{i}
, there exists a position
n_{i+1}
such that

x_{n_{i+1}}\geq x_{n_{i}}
.
The subsequence
x_{n_{1}},~x_{n_{2}},~x_{n_{3}},\dots
is monotone non-decreasing, and so,
convergent using Prop.
C.4.8
.

2.

If
N
is not finite, then
N
contains an infinite sequence of indices

n_{1}<n_{2}<n_{3}<\dots
such that
x_{n_{1}}>x_{n_{2}}>x_{n_{3}}\dots
. The
subsequence
x_{n_{1}},~x_{n_{2}},~x_{n_{3}}\dots
is monotone decreasing, and using
Prop.
C.4.8
, it is convergent.

3.

If
|N|=t
with
t\in\mathbb{N}-\{0\}
, then

N
contains a finite sequence of indices

n_{1}<n_{2}<n_{3}<\dots n_{t}
such that
x_{n_{1}}>x_{n_{2}}>x_{n_{3}}\dots>x_{n_{t}}
.
The set
N_{>n_{t}}=\left\{i\in\left\{n_{t}+1,n_{t}+2,n_{t}+3,\dots\right\}:~x_{j}<x_{i},\forall j>i\right\}
is empty.
We can thus apply the argument of case 1 and obtain that the infinite sequence

x_{n_{t}+1}
,
x_{n_{t}+2}
,
x_{n_{t}+3}
,
\dots
contains a convergent sub-sequence.

∎

We now generalize the result for any
n>1
. Considering the first position of
the sequence
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
, the above lemma shows that
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
contains a
sub-sequence
\{\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i}\}
whose first position converges to some
y_{1}
. We now
consider the second position of
\{\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i}\}
. Using the lemma again, we obtain
that
\{\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i}\}
contains a sub-sequence
\{\mathbf{x}^{2}_{i}\}
whose second position
converges to some
y_{2}
. We observe that the first position of
\{\mathbf{x}^{2}_{i}\}

converges to
y_{1}
and the second to
y_{2}
. The argument can be repeated to find
sub-sequences
\{\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i}\}
,
\{\mathbf{x}^{2}_{i}\}
,
\dots
,
\{\mathbf{x}^{n}_{i}\}
such that

\{\mathbf{x}^{n}_{i}\}
converges to
[y_{1}~y_{2}~\dots y_{n}]^{\top}
.
∎
